**English** | [繁體中文](developer-guide.zh-TW.md)

# Developer guide

How the code is organized, how to run and test it, and how to extend it. Read [Architecture](architecture.md) first for the big picture.

## Stack

| Layer | Tech |
| --- | --- |
| Language | TypeScript (strict), ES modules |
| Server | Node.js ≥ 22.13, [Hono](https://hono.dev) on `@hono/node-server`, run with `tsx` (no build step) |
| Database | SQLite via the built-in `node:sqlite` (`DatabaseSync`) |
| API keys | OS credential store via [`@napi-rs/keyring`](https://github.com/Brooooooklyn/keyring-node) (prebuilt binaries, no compiler needed) |
| Client | React 19, Vite, `react-markdown` + `remark-gfm` + `rehype-highlight` |
| Tests | Vitest |

## Project layout

```text
src/
  shared/types.ts          types shared by server and client (+ PROVIDERS, effortsFor…)
  shared/apps.ts           Common apps catalog, hosted connector IDs and draft availability states
  shared/connectorAccounts.ts  labels and identities for mail and calendar accounts
  shared/mentions.ts       the @mention rule shared by the server and the UI (Markdown prose only), and the names no member has
  shared/links.ts          where a bare URL ends, also right before Chinese text written after it (server and UI)
  shared/modelLimits.ts    an endpoint model's context window and output limit: what Settings takes, and what a run uses (the user's over the server's)
  shared/search.ts         search words, matches ignoring case, excerpts, highlighting (server and UI)
  shared/family.ts         families of branched conversations: each one's parent and top, also once the top is deleted (server and UI)
  server/tailscale.ts       private HTTPS phone address from Tailscale Serve, also used by the desktop app to set up sharing
  shared/auth.ts           signing a browser in (server and UI): one-time codes (Crockford base 32), the link's #code= fragment, a browser's label, the key header
  shared/share.ts          a shared page's placeholder links to the files that go along (written by the UI, filled in by the server), and sizes as the share limits count them
  shared/schedule.ts       when a schedule sends next: wall-clock times in a time zone, clock changes (server and UI)
  shared/scheduleProposal.ts  a schedule an agent proposes in its reply: its <schedule> block taken out, read, hidden while it streams; made in a time zone (server and UI)
  shared/tracking.ts       a tracked goal: a reply's <progress> block, the notes kept, the waits before each retry (server and UI)
  shared/memory.ts         long-term memory: a reply's <remember> and <forget> blocks, hiding them while it streams, the words notes are matched by, the pinned and recalled notes (server and UI)
  shared/handoffs.ts       secrets and fixed commands: a reply's <secret>, <command> and <command-run> blocks, hiding them while it streams, the checks of names, hosts and folders, masking (server and UI)
  shared/steps.ts          steps for the user: a reply's <steps> block, hiding it while it streams, the user's answer as their message says it (server and UI)
  shared/review.ts         lead review: where the next review starts, finding the words a review quotes in a reply (server and UI), each reply's marks
  shared/notify.ts         alerts: what changed in a conversation that the user is told of (a reply done, needs you), and its text's detail (server and UI)
  shared/diff.ts           the note that ends a diff the server cut short (written by the server, told apart by the diff dialog)
  shared/fileNames.ts      the names the Files panel gives a new or uploaded file, checked alike by the server and the page
  shared/cliVersions.ts    the version of each agent CLI Harness Relay was last checked with: one place, for Settings' installs of the open agents, the user guide and scripts/cli-check.ts
  server/
    index.ts               wiring, static files, startup and shutdown
    web.ts                 serves the built UI: hashed assets cached for good, index.html checked every time; where it is (RELAY_WEB_DIR)
    parent.ts              the desktop app that started the server, if any: tells it the server listens (or cannot), hears it ask for a shutdown, and what it says of its updates
    updates.ts             the desktop app's updates as the chat shows them: what the app last said, and the chat's asks (check now, update) passed on to it
    app.ts                 createApp(): routes, security middleware (Host/Origin, JSON writes, sign-in), SSE (testable with app.request())
    auth.ts                signed-in browsers: one-time codes, sessions (a cookie and a key, only their hashes stored), wrong-code limit
    push.ts                push notifications: browsers' subscriptions, VAPID keys (RFC 8292), message encryption (RFC 8291), sending to push services
    alerts.ts              tells subscribed browsers when agents finish or need the user, holding them while the browser's page is in front
    inbox.ts               the notification inbox (the sidebar's bell): every alert the team gives kept in the database (notifications), read by the rule push follows
    signin.ts              the sign-in link the server and `npm run login` print
    protect.ts             what agents below full access may not reach: the browsers' folders and the database with the secrets' key file (protectedReads), Harness Relay's own folder in edit mode
    orchestrator.ts        team turns: who speaks next, prompt delivery, streaming into messages; auto team, resend
    planner.ts             auto team: lead-agent prompt (question, history, current team, each CLI's models and tools, the tool library), reply parsing, runnable team, fallback;
                           changing a team at the user's request (revise prompt, `decideRevision`)
    roleGuides.ts          how each model family takes a role best, for the roles the lead agent writes (only the families its CLIs can run)
    grouping.ts            sidebar groups and titles: the lead agent sorts a new conversation by its first message and names it (one run, AutoGrouper);
                           its suggestions for organizing the groups (prompt, decideOrganize), and checking the ones the user took (checkReorganize)
    leadDrafts.ts          the lead agent's drafts for the editors that are not a conversation's team (LeadDrafts): a template, team checks, a schedule,
                           the sidebar's group suggestions; one read-only, fresh run each, in the project or an empty folder, a new one stopping the last
    checksDraft.ts         team checks the lead agent writes: its prompt (the project read first), the list checked rule by rule (decideChecks)
    scheduleDraft.ts       a schedule the lead agent writes for the schedule form: its prompt (the time in the user's zone, the accounts), decideSchedule
    memory.ts              long-term memory: what replies' blocks do to the notes, the user's changes, what a turn is given, the lead agent tidying the notes (its prompt, its changes applied)
    reviewer.ts            lead review: what the lead agent reads of the team's replies, its prompt, its reply turned into a review
    scheduler.ts           schedules: sends each conversation's scheduled message when it comes due, missed times, notes; checks what the automations that start when something happens wait for (watchTick, check)
    idle.ts                IMAP IDLE for the automations that wait for new mail: a connection for each account and folder, checking its automations when the server says mail arrived; renewed, opened again after a drop or a sleep, backing off (IdleWatcher)
    watch.ts               what such an automation waits for, checked by Harness Relay itself (the first check only records): a new mail (checkMail: UIDVALIDITY and the highest UID seen), an event about to begin (checkCalendar), a pull request or a failed CI run through gh (checkGithub), a file in the working directory (checkFiles, patternWalk, patternRegex; only recorded while a run writes there); the <event> blocks' bodies within 20 KB (fitEvent), the next check and its backoff
    prompt.ts              role briefing, turn prompt, @mention parsing, [PASS]
    approvals.ts           "always allow" rule keys (one per command the line runs)
    gateway.ts             Harness Relay's own tools: the gateway's socket and loopback HTTP endpoint, each turn's grant (token, MCP server spec), tiers (read, act, send), calls
    gatewayShim.ts         the MCP server the CLIs start for them (a copy for each turn, beside its token in data/tmp): MCP on stdio, calls over the socket
    runCode.ts             run_code (Settings → Labs): an agent's JavaScript that calls the turn's other tools, its runner in Harness Relay's own sandbox with no network, its calls over its own stdio, its limits
    llmQuery.ts            RLM mode's sub-model (Settings → Labs): one plain completion with no tools on a model connection of Settings, the only inference request the server makes itself
    cliSubModel.ts         RLM mode's sub-model through a CLI (cli:<provider>[:<account>][/<model>]): one toolless, read-only turn of Claude Code, Copilot or Muse Code as one of the user's accounts, in an empty folder of its own (RunOptions.toolless)
    outbox.ts              the outbox for outgoing messages, Google Docs changes and Obsidian note proposals. Each waits for the user to confirm or discard it
    handoffs.ts            secrets and fixed commands by working directory: the secrets' values in the credential store, their names, the commands and their pins in app_settings
    fixedCommands.ts       a fixed command's pin (the code in its folder) and its run in Harness Relay's own sandbox: its hosts only, its secrets in a file, its output masked
    interactive.ts         interactive commands: an <interactive> card's run, started by the user only, with the user's rights in a folder of its own, its output masked, typing as typed, ended by Cancel, its time limit or the server
    askpassShim.ts         the password helper an interactive command's `sudo -A`, ssh or git asks, over the gateway's socket with its session's token
    browser/               the browser tools: their own Chromium per conversation over its debugging pipe (`cdp.ts`), which browser (`find.ts`), what it may open, reading pages, clicks, typing and screenshots (`session.ts`), the proxy every connection goes through (`proxy.ts`), the tools on the gateway (`tools.ts`)
    browser/install.ts     browser prerequisite installation and platform checks on the Relay host
    browser/handover.ts    the hand-over of the chat's browser (`browser_hand_over`): its card, its wait and outcome, the window or the screencast and its input
    connectors/            Common apps server tools. Mail and calendar use the accounts (`accounts.ts`, passwords in the credential store), IMAP, SMTP, MIME and CalDAV of Harness Relay's own (`imap.ts`, `smtp.ts`: with a password, or an OAuth account's token over SASL XOAUTH2; `mime.ts`; `caldav.ts`: with a password, or an OAuth account's fetch, `CalDavAuth`), a mail account's servers and sign-in in one place (`mailLogin.ts`, `mailLogin()`: its password, or its provider's servers and a token for mail, a refused token renewed once), Microsoft Graph's calendars (`graph.ts`), one calendar however it is reached (`calendar.ts`, `calendarBackend()`), trying an account (`test.ts`), the tools on the gateway (`tools.ts`)
      apps.ts, driveDocuments.ts, obsidian.ts   GitHub and app tools, Google Docs writes, Obsidian vaults and note proposals
      hostedApps.ts, codexHostedProfile.ts, draftAvailability.ts  hosted Claude and Codex connectors, Slack profile verification, app readiness for drafts
      messaging.ts, slack.ts, teams.ts, teamsPowerShell.ts, teamsRuntime.ts  message proposals and delivery, Microsoft Graph sessions and the PowerShell prerequisite
    appDependencies.ts, githubInstall.ts, portableInstall.ts   shared prerequisite installation jobs, portable GitHub CLI and verified archive installation
    oauth/                 accounts that sign in with OAuth: the providers' descriptions (`providers.ts`: Microsoft, Google, with their scopes for each use and their own mail servers; Harness Relay's own apps, none yet), the accounts and their apps (`store.ts`, refresh tokens in the credential store), the token broker (`broker.ts`: a token for each use where a provider's are each for one resource, refreshed early and one at a time per account, the host rule, no redirects), the sign-ins (`signin.ts`: PKCE and the loopback redirect, a pasted address, the device code, the scopes of each use checked), what ties them to the calendar and mail tools (`service.ts`, `OAuthService`), and the tool library HTTP MCP servers' own sign-ins (`mcp.ts`, `McpOAuth`: discovery, registration, the mix-up checks, each turn's token; `net.ts`: their requests, for a server elsewhere only to addresses on the internet, checked and pinned)
    commands.ts            reads a shell command line: simple commands, sh -c, wrappers, publish check
    i18n.ts                server messages (zh-TW / en), language per request and run
    agents/
      types.ts             AgentRunner interface, AgentEvent, helpers
      claude.ts            Claude Code: stream-json both ways, can_use_tool control requests
      codex.ts             Codex: `codex app-server` (thread / turn / approval requests)
      grok.ts              Grok: ACP (`grok agent stdio`)
      cursor.ts            Cursor: ACP (`cursor-agent acp`), no OS sandbox; a private config folder per run (its settings, deny rules, a link to the account's sessions)
      antigravity.ts       Antigravity: ACP (`agy acp`), on a Gemini API key only (never a Google sign-in; not started without a key), no sandbox (below full access every command asks; a refusal ends its turn); settings of its own per run (`--cli_dir_name`: the account's model only, and `modelProvider: "gemini"`), a project's own MCP servers, hooks and plugins asked about before it starts, the commands it leaves running ended after it, the account's home (`--gemini-dir`), the briefing in its system prompt
      copilot.ts           Copilot: ACP (`copilot --acp`), its own sandbox below full access (`--sandbox`, its policy checked before each turn; a refusal ends its turn); a home of its own per run (`prepareCopilotRun`: Harness Relay's settings, the account's sign-in state, links to its sessions (`session-state`, and on macOS and Linux its `session-store.db`, whose usage AgentsView reads for token counts) and what it loads; in Copilot's usual home, `~/.agents/skills` as a skill folder), what decides how agents run later and the shared caches read-only in its sandbox, a project's own hooks asked about before it starts (and its sandbox checked first, in a home where nothing runs, when a session would run something by itself), the user's own endpoints through its BYOK variables, the briefing in its system prompt (`copilot-instructions.md`)
      muse.ts              Muse Code: ACP (`muse acp`), in its own OS sandbox below full access; a config folder of its own per run (`XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, in the data folder's `muse-runs`: an allowlist of the account's settings with Harness Relay's permission profile, from `:ask-me` with a deny rule per protected path; a link to the account's sign-in, and to its skills with a copy of its rules (`AGENTS.md`); a `trust.json` marking the working directories untrusted below full access), the session's mode per permission, a command's network always asked of the user, a session resumed only with the permission and protected paths it was made with, its approval judge off, the briefing as its developer prompt, the empty folders its Linux sandbox leaves in the working directories removed
      acp.ts               JSON-RPC peer used by the Codex, Grok, Cursor, Antigravity, Copilot and Muse Code adapters and the generic ACP turn, and the ACP helpers Grok, Cursor, Antigravity, Copilot and Muse Code share (prompt blocks, MCP servers, permission answers, content text)
      acpTurn.ts           the open agents' generic ACP turn: session updates → events, permission → decision → approval, resume, model / effort / mode, per-CLI hooks (`AcpAgentSpec`)
      open.ts              what the open agents share around their process: the run's backend, their home (state and config paths), the run's temp folder, the relay sandbox (`startOpenProcess`)
      opencode.ts goose.ts cline.ts openhands.ts
                           the ACP open agents, one file each: their `AcpAgentSpec` and `OpenAgentDefinition`
      pi.ts                Pi over its JSONL RPC (`pi --mode rpc`)
      piGate.js            Pi's gate extension (loaded by Pi below full access): asks before each tool call over the RPC
      index.ts             runner registry, and `openAgents`
    process.ts             spawn/kill helpers (process groups, line streaming, raw-byte capture, timeouts), runSync() for a quick probe the server waits for, converse() and converseFramed() for a short JSON-RPC exchange (JSON lines, or LSP's Content-Length frames: FrameDecoder)
    winLaunch.ts           on Windows, what to start for a CLI that is a .cmd (npm's shims, Cursor's and Muse Code's launchers)
    ports.ts               Harness Relay's ports: 8787 (npm start, the desktop app), 8789 and Vite's 5173 (npm run dev, or the next free ones), PORT over either
    config.ts              what the environment sets at startup: accounts per CLI, team size, the turn timeout, voice input's download folders and its own folder, where Microsoft's sign-in and Graph are (checked; the server refuses to start on a value that is not valid)
    share.ts               sharing a conversation: the page as a secret gist through the user's gh (create, delete), its link on the viewer (by the id alone for a gist address without its owner); the files its replies link to (found as the download route finds them, of any kind, named apart) in a second secret gist that git pushes them into, signed in by gh, found again by its token when gh cannot say it made it
    about.ts               which Harness Relay this is and on what (its version, the system, Node), for a problem report: nothing that names the user
    screenshots.ts         a problem report's screenshots: checked to be PNG, JPEG or WebP images (no GIF), named by their place, without their metadata (a photo's location), uploaded as one secret gist through the user's gh
    providers.ts           CLI status, login sessions and logout per account, model cache, each account's plan usage (kept a while)
    login.ts               a CLI's own sign-in run without a terminal (CliLogin): its output, links and codes, input, timeout; the CLIs' in Settings and gh's for sharing
    usage.ts               each account's plan usage, asked of its CLI without a prompt (Claude Code's get_usage, Codex's account/rateLimits/read, Grok's _x.ai/billing, muse usage --json, Copilot's account.getQuota on its headless server, Cursor's plan from about); none for an account on a key its provider bills by use, nor for Antigravity, which runs on a key only and is never asked
    models.ts              model / effort discovery per CLI
    db.ts                  open SQLite, migrations, transaction(), the data folder's lock (lockDataDir)
    store.ts               conversations, messages, agent sessions, presets, sidebar groups, search, schedules, the notification inbox
    settings.ts            provider settings, each CLI's accounts, default cwd, the CLIs' own homes, child-process env
    agentsview.ts          AgentsView: each conversation sent as one thread, the agents' sessions linked under it (Cursor's and the open agents' as Harness Relay recorded them)
    secrets.ts             OS credential store for API keys (keyring), availability check; without one, the database encrypted with the key in secrets.key (openSecretStore)
    backends.ts            the open agents' model backends: API keys and custom endpoints (keyring, `model_backends`) and the deleted endpoints' ids (`deleted_endpoints`), `backendRun()` for one run; `endpointRunModel()` for a run of Claude Code, Codex, Grok or Copilot on an endpoint, or refused on a deleted one
    endpointModels.ts      loads a custom endpoint’s model catalog and verifies model IDs against the catalog or individual model metadata before saving from Settings, reading the context window the server reports from the same answers
    contextWindow.ts       a run's endpoint model's context window and output limit, and the figures the adapters write from them (an answer's share, a tool output's, a short window)
    relaySandbox.ts        Harness Relay's own sandbox around the open agents below full access (`sandboxCommand`, srt)
    relaySandboxHost.ts    the host process each sandboxed run gets: one sandbox-runtime for that run, its proxy and its control channel (fd 3)
    sandboxProcs.ts        a relay-sandbox run's leftover processes on macOS, found by their sandbox (sandbox_check)
    fsRemove.ts            removing what a run left without following links; the owner's permissions given back only at a start whose sweeps found nothing left
    tools.ts               the tool library: MCP servers (secrets in the keyring; an HTTP server's own sign-in kept with it), skills (SKILL.md front matter), memory; what each agent gets
    previewFiles.ts        bounded app snapshots, public asset filtering and static HTTP responses
    files.ts               browsing a conversation's files: roots, listing, preview (and its version), path checks, what a folder's ZIP holds
    fileEdit.ts            changing files from the Files panel: the roots it writes in, a text file saved over its version, a new file, an upload; written at once without following links
    zip.ts                 a ZIP file written as it is sent (deflate, data descriptors, UTF-8 names)
    folderPicker.ts        the system's own folder dialog for the working directory box (AppleScript, zenity / kdialog, Windows Forms): which one, its command, its answer
    native.ts              what each CLI loads by itself (its skills, MCP servers and their tools, built-in tools, hooks, subagents, plugins, instruction and memory files), asked without a prompt (Cursor's read from the files it reads, as are Antigravity's MCP servers and instruction files, Copilot's hooks and custom agents, and Muse Code's MCP servers and hooks), and what Harness Relay's runs leave out of it
    grokTrust.ts           records Grok's trust in a folder (as grok --trust does), when the user confirms
    attachments.ts         files attached to messages: storage, safe names, images for the agents
    presets.ts             built-in team templates, in both languages
    validate.ts            input validation for agents, settings, schedules and model backends
    voice.ts               voice input: whisper.cpp engine and model installs (pinned, SHA-256 checked), the engine process, transcription
    cliInstall.ts          installing a CLI from Settings into a folder of its own (clis/<provider> in the data folder): with npm (for Claude Code, Codex and Copilot without one, Node.js's own build put in the folder first, its checksum checked), its maker's script (bash, or on Windows PowerShell or Git Bash, with that folder as its home) or uv (installed into the folder first when missing) (CLI_RECIPES), in the background, checked to run, the links it left outside leading into the folder taken out (on Windows the user's PATH put back as it was before its script), and removed again
    sandboxSetup.ts        what the agents' OS sandboxes need that this computer lacks (Linux packages with the package manager, Ubuntu's AppArmor profile for bwrap, the desktop app's Node.js for Harness Relay's own sandbox), installed in one run of a fixed script with administrator rights (root, sudo -n, pkexec), else the command to run by hand
    gitInstall.ts          Git installation on request, through Apple Command Line Tools, WinGet or the Linux package runner shared with sandbox setup
    gitProcess.ts          finding and running Git, including fresh Git for Windows installs on an older PATH and Apple Git without opening its installer stub
    workspace.ts           git: auto init, snapshots, worktrees, diffs, apply
    errors.ts              HttpError (user-facing errors with a status)
    hub.ts                 SSE fan-out
  web/
    main.tsx App.tsx api.ts util.ts copy.ts styles.css index.html
    auth.ts                the key a signed-in browser sends (localStorage, this origin only); what an answer "signed out" means (after the sign-ins on their way in this tab: signed out only for the key the request was sent with, which stays in the storage for the other tabs; sent again, once, with a key a sign-in put in its place; else left alone); another tab keeping a new key (`keyArrived`)
    signin.ts              signing in with a link (the code taken out of the address, the page's first check, shared by both runs of App's effect in development) and where the sign-in page points
    public/                copied into the build as it is: manifest.webmanifest and the home-screen icons (npm run icons), and sw.js, the service worker that shows push notifications (written by hand)
    alerts.ts              notifications and sound when agents finish or need the user (Settings → Notifications and sound): the browser's choices, what it can do, what the page does in each situation, one tab per alert, the push subscription kept in step, the sound
    inbox.ts               the notification inbox in the page: the bell's count, which chat's notifications are read as it is on screen, each one's line and mark
    chat.ts                ChatView's pure logic: stream reconnect decision, Apply availability, team-drawer draft and the team it edits, when the messages follow new output and when the button to their end shows
    diff.ts                the diff dialog's files: git's diff split into a section per file (its path, however git writes it, status and counts), a file whose type changed as one, where the server cut it short, each line's colour
    review.ts              the words a lead review quotes, marked in their replies (CSS Custom Highlight API), and jumping between a reply and its review
    sidebar.ts             the sidebar's pure logic: conversations in their groups, each family of branches as a tree, pending moves, the pinned conversations and pending pins, a collapsed group's or family's state
    swipe.ts               the drawer sidebar's swipe on a phone or tablet (right opens it, left closes it, a quick flick too): which touches may swipe, when a move is one, when a box that scrolls sideways takes it (useSidebarDrawer gives it the touches)
    schedule.ts            the schedule drawer's pure logic: the form, its problems, the next times, a schedule in a few words (what it waits for, how its checks go)
    oauth.ts               accounts that sign in with OAuth, in the page: the redirect addresses a provider's app must allow, what the provider's page sent back to /oauth/callback, a calendar's name, what each account offers for calendars and mail, the uses a new sign-in asks for, what an MCP server's row says of its sign-in, what it asks before a sign-in page elsewhere opens, and whether an edit names another server
    templates.ts           the template editor's pure logic: whether a template was changed, what the lead agent changed of its name and description
    groups.ts              the sidebar's "Organize groups" dialog: the lead agent's suggestions ticked or not, and what applying them sends
    files.ts               the file browser's pure logic: breadcrumb, filter, code language, line count, a folder's ZIP choice
    fileEdit.ts            the Files panel's editing and uploading: when it offers to edit, what a text box holds, the save key, what a pick or drop of files sends
    cwds.ts                the working directories box's pure logic: which working directories in use match what is typed (`~` as the home folder, `/` and `\` alike), how its list answers the keys and its arrow button, and its chips (adding each folder once, Enter and Backspace, where the folder dialog opens)
    download.ts            whether a file a reply links to can be downloaded (a HEAD request, a moment after it appears), and what a click does while it is missing
    share.ts               a shared page's DOM-free parts: what it leaves out, the image budget, the document and its CSP, the Harness Relay bar; the files its replies link to (read from download links, what each link becomes, which are chosen and kept across checks, which look secret)
    siteDocs.ts            links to the website's docs for people (site/docs/) from a section of Settings whose setup takes steps elsewhere, in the page's language: the Render and Neon guides
    update.ts              the desktop app's updates in the chat: the dialog offering a new version once (in the app's own window, while no other dialog is open), Settings' Version card, how often to ask; and the page's watch on the server's version, which reloads a page open across an update
    dialogs.ts             the dialogs open on the page: only the top one takes keys (useDialog), and how many are open
    star.ts                Harness Relay's repository on GitHub (REPO, REPO_URL), which the star dialog opens: the landing page's REPO
    report.ts              a problem report by email: the mail's subject and text, the details that go along, the link to its screenshots, the mailto and Gmail links (and the clipboard for a report too long for them)
    sharePage.tsx          makes a shared page from the chat on screen (copy, strip, styles, images as data URLs within 10 MB, placeholder links to the files that go along); the file links of the shared messages
    tools.ts               the team editor's tool picker: library items, defaults per CLI, an agent's exceptions
    memory.ts              Settings → Labs → Long-term memory's pure logic: notes by state, searched, folder names and choices, who wrote a note, which note replaced one, what it says of tidying
    steps.ts               a steps card's pure logic (StepsCard): where it stands, the boxes while ticks are on their way, the answer it sends, a step in a few words, the steps as a task list
    handoffs.ts            the secrets, fixed command and interactive command cards' pure logic (HandoffCards): what the user typed, what a card offers, how a run reads
    handoffs.css           those cards' styles, and Settings → Secrets and commands'
    handover.ts            a browser hand-over's pure logic (HandoverCard): coordinates and keys for the screencast, a page's host
    backends.ts            Settings → Model connections: the endpoint form's pure logic (an id from the name, selected or manually entered model IDs, each model's context window and output limit as typed, its problems)
    labs.ts                Settings → Labs: its pure logic (the model connections a sub-model can be on, the CLI accounts it can be asked through and their models, the model box's suggestions, what Save sends or why it cannot, whether there is something to save)
    memberLimits.ts        a member's own limit in the team editor: which CLIs have one, its unit and bounds, what is typed read
    native.ts              NativeSetupPanel's pure logic: each CLI's tabs and their counts, ticking single items, what its runs leave out, whether it changed; and the Subagents and workflows box's (CliFeatures)
    usage.ts               UsagePanel's pure logic: a limit's name, what is left of it, its bar's colour, when it starts over, the credits, why an account has no limits
    route.ts               the client's routes in the URL hash: #/ (home), #/new (New chat), #/schedule, #/c/<id> and #/settings/<section>
    voice.ts               voice input's pure logic: WAV encoding, silence, inserting the transcript, install progress
    recorder.ts            microphone recording in the browser, decoded to 16 kHz mono
    useVoice.ts            the voice status hook (polls while an install runs)
    clis.ts                installing a CLI from its card: what the card shows (install, why it cannot, under way, Harness Relay's with Remove), a status checked before the install, the installs that ended
    useCliInstalls.ts      the CLIs' installs hook (polls while one runs; tells the page when one ended)
    sandboxSetup.ts        the sandboxes' setup on the page: whether New chat asks (once per browser), what Install does, what to run by hand
    useSandboxSetup.ts     the sandboxes' setup hook (polls while its install runs)
    i18n.tsx               language detection, provider, useT(); each language's messages loaded when needed (loadMessages)
    highlight.ts           the file browser's highlighting, with the highlighter replies' code blocks use
    tour.ts                the guided tour's topics, which this browser read, and whether to open or ask about it (the tour itself loads when it opens)
    chunks.ts              a part of the page loaded when first needed that could not be (a page open across an update): the page reloads once for it, not again within a minute (loadPart)
    limits.ts              the server's limits (GET /api/limits: accounts per CLI, team size, the turn timeout), the defaults until they arrive, asked again when a component opens, a chat's live updates reconnect, the sidebar reaches the server again or the page is shown again (only while a component shows them: never on the sign-in page)
    refresh.ts             the CLIs' status as the server checked it last (newerStatuses), and loads of what it does not date, such as the model catalog, in the order asked for (latestLoader)
    theme.ts               the light or dark theme: the system's, or the one chosen in Settings → Appearance (data-theme, kept in localStorage)
    locales/               zh-TW.ts (source of the message shape), en.ts
    draftApps.ts, draftAppStatus.ts, useDraftApps.ts   draft app selections, per-permission readiness cache and verification on submission
    notePreview.ts         Obsidian note preview change blocks, BOM and line-ending summaries
    components/            Sidebar, SidebarGroups (groups, pins, drag and drop), SidebarSearch,
                           Home (the animated logo and the tagline, where the app opens), NewChat, TemplateEditor (the template dialog), TeamEditor, TeamReviser (the team
                           drawer's "Ask the lead agent"), LeadAsk (the lead agent's box the team drawer, the template editor, team checks and the
                           schedule form share), GroupOrganizer (the sidebar's "Organize groups"), ModelPicker, CliPicker (a choice of CLI: one row that scrolls sideways), ChatView,
                           MessageItem (incl. plan card), ChangesCard, Composer, Markdown,
                           SettingsPage, UsagePanel (an account's plan usage in Settings), CliFeatures (an account's subagents and workflows), CopyButton, Logo, Onboarding (guided tour), TourArt (each topic's picture), TourNudge (the home page's question about the topics left),
                           MemberDots, icons, VoiceInput (microphone, setup dialog, install progress),
                           VoiceSettings (Settings → Voice input), LabsSettings (Settings → Labs, with long-term memory below its modes), ToolsSettings (Settings → Apps and tools, with the Common apps and Tool library tabs),
                           LongTermMemory (Labs → Long-term memory),
                           StepsCard (an agent's steps for the user to take outside Harness Relay, ticked and answered in the chat),
                           HandoffCards (the secrets, fixed command and interactive command cards), HandoffsSettings (Settings → Secrets and commands), HandoverCard (a browser handed to the user: its card, the screencast's panel),
                           CliInstall (a CLI's install and removal on its card in Settings),
                           SandboxSetup (Settings → Sandboxes), SandboxSetupDialog (New chat's dialog),
                           BackendsSettings (Settings → Model connections),
                           SessionsSettings (Settings → Agent sessions),
                           SharesSettings (Settings → Shared conversations), ShareDialog (copy or share a conversation),
                           SignIn (the sign-in page), BrowsersSettings (signed-in sessions), HomeScreenGuide and PhonePairing (Settings → Connect your phone),
                           NotificationsSettings (Settings → Notifications and sound),
                           InboxButton (the bell beside the sidebar's collapse button), InboxDialog (the notification inbox it opens),
                           GhHelp (what to do when the GitHub CLI is missing or not signed in, and its sign-in from there), LoginLinks (a sign-in's link and code, there and in Settings),
                           FileBrowser (the Files dialog), FileEditing (its editor, New file and uploads; filesEdit.css), NativeSetupPanel (what a CLI loads by itself),
                           Deferred (a part loaded when first shown, lazyPart: a note to reload the page when it cannot be loaded or drawn),
                           GrokTrust (Grok's trust in a folder), SectionTitle,
                           CwdInput (the working directories box: a chip per folder, the Enter key after what is typed, the folder button, and the list of the working directories in use),
                           ScheduleDrawer (a conversation's schedule, Check now), ScheduleForm (the schedule's form, shared; When something happens, each thing it waits for: TriggerFields),
                           CommonApps, AppRow, SlackAppRow, HostedAppLinks, SlackPostingSettings, TeamsSignIn, AppDependencyInstall,
                           DocumentPreview, NotePreview (outbox previews of Google Docs and Obsidian note changes),
                           OAuthAccounts (OAuth accounts in Common apps, plus provider registration, enablement and account disconnection in Account sign-in settings), McpSignIn (an HTTP MCP server's own sign-in, in its row), OAuthCallback (the page a provider's sign-in comes back to, /oauth/callback),
                           NewSchedule (the New automation page),
                           ReportDialog (the sidebar's Report a problem), StarDialog (the star beside it: star the repository on GitHub),
                           VersionSettings (Settings → Version), UpdateDialog (a new version, offered once),
                           useDialog (focus handling for dialogs), useSidebarDrawer (the sidebar drawer's touches for its swipe, src/web/swipe.ts, and closing it on a wide screen)
test/                      Vitest tests; fixtures/<agent>/ holds messages recorded from the open agents', Antigravity's, Copilot's and Muse Code's real CLIs (fixtures/antigravity/cli/ and fixtures/muse/cli/: agy's and muse's own listings; fixtures/copilot/cli/: copilot's; fixtures/usage/: each CLI's answer about an account's plan usage, none of Antigravity's, which is never asked; fixtures/cli-check/<cli>/: the shapes of what each CLI said in scripts/cli-check.ts's layers 2 and 3, for the version checked), fake-*.ts the stand-ins that replay them (fake-antigravity.ts for antigravity.test.ts and Antigravity's accounts in providers.test.ts, fake-copilot.ts for copilot.test.ts, fake-muse.ts for muse.test.ts and Muse Code's sign-in in providers.test.ts); fake-mail-servers.ts: a mail account's IMAP (with UIDVALIDITY, UIDNEXT and UID ranges), SMTP and CalDAV servers on this computer, the first two taking XOAUTH2 with the tokens a test says; fake-oauth-server.ts: Microsoft's sign-in (a token for one resource, Graph's or Exchange Online's) and Graph, Google's sign-in (incremental consent), userinfo and CalDAV (with a token only); fake-mcp-auth-server.ts: an MCP server that signs in with OAuth and its authorization server; setup.ts and timeouts.ts: how long tests wait (longer on Windows); orchestrator-harness.ts: the fake CLI the orchestrator's tests share; workflow-steps.ts: the Release and Pages workflows' steps (and a workflow's jobs), for the tests that run or read them; site-pages.ts: the website's pages, in the sitemap's order, for the site tests; pgUri.ts: a Postgres address with a made-up password put in as the test runs, since GitHub's secret scanning reports one written out in the source as a leaked connection string
e2e/                       end-to-end tests (Playwright): npm run test:e2e
  README.md                the scenarios they cover, spec by spec (README.zh-TW.md in Chinese)
  fake-cli/fake-cli.cjs    the fake CLIs' entry: which of the twelve this process is, by the name it runs under
  fake-cli/agents/         one fake per CLI (<provider>.cjs), in its own protocol; opencode-goose.cjs what those two share
  fake-cli/lib/            what the fakes share: ACP and JSON-RPC on stdio, a turn's steps (plan.cjs: the e2e: directives), homes, files, the log
  fake-cli/fake-dialog.cjs the fake folder dialog (zenity, for Linux)
  fake-cli/fake-pwsh.cjs   PowerShell and process-local Microsoft Graph device sign-in, channel reads and posting for Teams tests
  support/                 a server for each test and its fake model endpoint (relay.ts), installing the fakes, bundled into one script (fake-cli.ts), the UI steps (app.ts), whether Harness Relay's own sandbox works here (sandbox.ts)
  support/clis.ts          the CLIs as the specs see them: eachCli(), the two teams (HALVES, eachHalf()), the CLIs a test server has (withClis()), the systems a CLI's tests run on (E2E_SYSTEM_CLIS), the permission each runs with here, skips (needsBelowFull, needsRelaySandbox), homes and sessions
  support/clis/            each CLI's facts for the specs (<provider>.ts, changed with its fake), all.ts gathers them
  fixtures.ts              the relay and page fixtures; global-setup.ts builds the UI (and a development build of it) and installs the fakes
  specs/                   the tests
  live/                    the live tests: the real CLIs on real models (npm run test:e2e:live; never on CI)
playwright.config.ts       the end-to-end tests' configuration (E2E_BROWSERS)
playwright.live.config.ts  the live tests' configuration
.gitattributes             every text file checked out with LF, also on Windows
.devcontainer/             the dev container for GitHub Codespaces (docs/codespaces.md): setup.sh installs the sandboxes' packages and the seven CLIs that are not open source, start.sh starts the server on the codespace's address, count.sh counts the codespace once at the download mirror (codespace.sh: what both read of Codespaces' variables)
.github/workflows/ci.yml   CI: typecheck, tests and build on pull requests and pushes to main, on Linux and Windows; the desktop app packaged for Linux and checked
.github/workflows/e2e.yml  the end-to-end tests on Linux, macOS and Windows (for pull requests and release candidates)
.github/workflows/pages.yml  publishes site/ to GitHub Pages
.github/workflows/cli-versions.yml  every day: the agent CLIs' newest versions against the ones checked, each new version checked once on Linux, and an issue (label cli-check) only when that check found a failed check or something removed
.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/    the issue forms (bug report, feature request; both languages in each) and the links beside them
.github/pull_request_template.md  what a pull request's description covers (both languages)
scripts/oauth-guides.ts    generates the bundled Google and Microsoft OAuth guides from the bilingual Tools pages
scripts/preview-site.ts    npm run site:preview: serves site/ locally like Pages
scripts/sandbox-check.ts   npx tsx scripts/sandbox-check.ts: real checks of Harness Relay's own sandbox on this computer (not part of npm test)
scripts/mcp-headers-check.ts  npx tsx scripts/mcp-headers-check.ts <cli>: whether a real CLI, signed in here, writes an HTTP MCP server's headers to a file (not part of npm test)
scripts/cli-check.ts       npx tsx scripts/cli-check.ts [<cli>]: a CLI's new version checked against what its adapter relies on, in layers (scripts/cli-check/: args, versions and issues, probe, shape, model, scenarios, changelog, layers, scratch, and for CI obtain and ci; not part of npm test)
scripts/app-icons.ts       npm run icons: draws the home-screen icons and writes the web app manifest
scripts/ci-scope.ts        which of CI's slower jobs a change needs (the tests on Windows, the packaged app, the end-to-end tests): the first job of ci.yml and e2e.yml
scripts/release.ts         the release version rules and the notes' list of changes: npm run release:next, release:check, release:notes, the Release workflow
scripts/stats.ts           npm run stats: the releases' downloads (GitHub's counts and about how many downloads, new installs and updates) by system and release, and the codespaces count, for maintainers
.github/workflows/release.yml  the Release workflow (run by hand): checks, five installers, one GitHub release (and a copy of each for the app's updates, in its own repository)
scripts/dev.ts             npm run dev: finds the API's and Vite's ports (8789 and 5173, or the first free ones after them: scripts/dev/ports.ts), then runs the two with concurrently (scripts/dev/concurrently.ts)
scripts/demo/              npm run demo: records the demo again and updates its videos, stills and review notes
  recorder.ts              what every recording shares: its own server, the headless browser, frames and marks
  use-cases/               npm run use-cases: records the website's twelve use cases again (their workspaces, what each
                           types and clicks, their chapters) and publishes their videos, posters and rails
site/                      the website (static HTML, English + 繁體中文): the landing page and the use-cases page
                           (use-cases/); DESIGN-GUIDELINES.md is not served
mirror/                    the website's download mirror: a Cloudflare Worker (worker.ts) in front of an R2 bucket, and its
                           wrangler.toml; deployed by hand (Releasing, "The download mirror")
docs/                      documentation (English + 繁體中文)
data/                      runtime data (relay.db) — git-ignored
desktop/                   the desktop app (Electron), a package of its own: npm install --prefix desktop
  README.md                the app's page for developers (README.zh-TW.md in Chinese)
  src/main.ts              the app: its window, its tray menu, starting and stopping the server, open at login, sharing with
                           the tailnet, updates, and its own check (--smoke-test); the only file that imports electron
  src/supervisor.ts        starts, watches and stops the server process (tested with a fake one)
  src/env.ts               the login shell's environment, and the server's (data folder, port, names it answers to)
  src/autostart.ts         open at login on Linux (an XDG autostart entry)
  src/prefs.ts             the app's own settings (desktop.json)
  src/i18n.ts              the app's menus and dialogs in both languages, in the one the chat in its window shows
  src/icons.ts             the app and tray icons, drawn at build time from the mark
  src/manifest.ts          the package.json of the app folder Electron runs
  src/lifecycle.ts         the app's decisions without Electron: opening it again, crash notices, the chat's address, login items,
                           where the window's links go (guardContents) and what its pages may use, the tailnet name
  src/profile.ts           the window's browser folder (browser/), kept from agents; an old sign-in beside it removed
  src/log.ts               the server's log, started over past 5 MB (also while it runs)
  src/update.ts            updates to GitHub's newest release (a release candidate too, for an app that is one): the check, the download and its SHA-256,
                           the wait for the conversations, putting it in place
  src/updateSource.ts      where the app downloads its updates from: a repository and, for a private one, a token, fixed when it is built;
                           the Release workflow's checks that the repository can be read before anything is built, that the installers
                           were built with them, and that an app can download the copies
  src/traymenu.ts          the tray menu while it is open: changed in place, and opened again to show what changed
  src/selftest.ts          what the app needs of the server over HTTP, a question to the fake CLIs included (smoke.ts and the app's --smoke-test)
  scripts/build.ts         bundles the server and the app into desktop/build/ (as RELAY_VERSION, for a release; RELAY_UPDATES_REPO, RELAY_UPDATES_TOKEN)
  scripts/smoke.ts         runs the bundled server as the app does and checks it (CI, Release workflow)
  scripts/dist.ts          npm run desktop:dist: the installer, signed ad hoc, and what to double-click
  scripts/check-package.ts checks the packaged app: its fuses, its resources, no token in the server's files nor as text in the app's, the update source its environment names (encoded), and its own check (--smoke-test)
  scripts/packaged.ts      finds the packaged apps electron-builder left next to the installers
  entitlements.mac.plist   the Mac app's entitlements (the microphone, for voice input)
  macos/                   the microphone's reason in each language (InfoPlist.strings)
  scripts/parent-port.mjs  Electron's process.parentPort over Node's IPC, for smoke.ts and the tests
  electron-builder.yml     the installers (npm run desktop:dist), into desktop/release/
```

## Running

```bash
npm install
npm run dev        # tsx watch (API :8789) + vite (UI :5173, proxies /api/ to the API); prints a sign-in link for :5173
                   # either port taken (another checkout's npm run dev, say): the next free one, said in a [dev] line
npm run format     # Prettier, the desktop app included (.prettierrc.json; .prettierignore: what it leaves alone)
npm run format:check   # the same check as CI: changes nothing, fails on a file Prettier would change
npm run typecheck
npm test
npm run test:e2e   # end-to-end tests in a browser, with fake CLIs (npm run test:e2e:install once)
E2E_LIVE_OLLAMA=http://127.0.0.1:11434/v1 npm run test:e2e:live   # the same with the real CLIs on your own models (see Live tests)
npm run build      # UI → dist/web
npm start          # one process: API + built UI on :8787; prints a sign-in link
npm run login      # another one-time sign-in link (ten minutes)
npx tsx scripts/sandbox-check.ts   # Harness Relay's own sandbox for real on this computer (macOS; Linux with bubblewrap, socat, ripgrep)
                                   # --only <text>: the checks whose name contains it; --keep: keep its scratch folder
                                   # run it after changing relaySandbox.ts, relaySandboxHost.ts, sandboxProcs.ts or an adapter's sandbox needs
npx tsx scripts/mcp-headers-check.ts opencode   # whether a real CLI keeps an HTTP MCP server's headers in memory only, before it joins GATEWAY_OVER_HTTP
npx tsx scripts/cli-check.ts       # each CLI here newer than the version it was checked with, checked in layers (see Checking a CLI's new version)
npm run site:preview   # the website (site/) on 127.0.0.1:4180, as GitHub Pages serves it
npm run icons          # redraw the home-screen icons and the web app manifest in src/web/public/
npm run demo           # record the demo again with the real CLIs (see Recording the demo)
npm run use-cases      # record the website's use cases again with the real CLIs (see Recording the use cases)

npm install --prefix desktop   # once, for the desktop app only: Electron and electron-builder
npm run desktop                # build the UI and the desktop app, and run it without installing it
npm run desktop:dist           # …and make the installer for this system in desktop/release/
npm run desktop:dist -- --output <folder>   # …elsewhere (the `--` hands the option to the script)
npx tsx desktop/scripts/check-package.ts    # check the packaged app, as a release is checked
npm run desktop:typecheck
```

`npm run build` puts the libraries the page loads at startup in their own chunks (`react`, `highlight`, `vendor`), so a browser keeps them across app updates; Mermaid stays lazy. It warns when a chunk loaded at startup passes 500 kB (`vite.config.ts`); Mermaid's lazy chunks are larger and only load when a diagram needs them. What the page does not need to show itself loads when first opened, not at startup: Settings and its panels and the guided tour (`React.lazy` in `App.tsx`), and the dialogs a chat or page opens (the file browser, a schedule, sharing, a template, reporting a problem); keep a large view that opens on demand lazy too. Only the language the page shows is loaded, before it is drawn (`loadMessages()` in `src/web/i18n.tsx`; the other when chosen), its file asked for by the page's head along with the page's own (`languagePreload` in `vite.config.ts`, for the language saved in the browser). Make a lazy part with `lazyPart` and show it in `Deferred` (`src/web/components/Deferred.tsx`), not with a bare `React.lazy` and `Suspense`: a part that cannot be loaded, as in a page left open across an update, has the page reload once, the part waiting meanwhile (`loadPart` in `src/web/chunks.ts`, which `loadMessages` uses too), then says so with a button to reload, instead of an empty page; a part that fails to draw says that instead. Only those parts reload the page: Mermaid, also loaded on demand, shows a diagram's source when it cannot be loaded. There is one copy of highlight.js: the file browser highlights with lowlight, as replies' code blocks do (`src/web/highlight.ts`), and `highlight.js` (for its theme) stays on the range `lowlight` takes, or npm installs a second copy.

The web app manifest and the icons a phone puts on its home screen (or a browser shows for the installed app) are in `src/web/public/`. They are drawn by `npm run icons` (`scripts/app-icons.ts`) from the same three circles as the favicon and `Logo.tsx`, without an image library; run it again after changing the mark, the icons' sizes or the manifest, and commit what it writes. `src/web/public/sw.js`, the service worker that shows push notifications, is written by hand: plain JavaScript, not built, registered only once a browser turns notifications on ([Architecture → Notifications](architecture.md#notifications)).

`npm run dev` restarts the server on every file change. A restart aborts any running agent turn (its message is marked aborted), so avoid editing server files while a long turn is running.

### Environment variables

| Variable | Default | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `PORT` | `8787` (`8789` with `npm run dev`, or the next free port) | API port (the Vite proxy uses it too). `npm run dev` has a port of its own so that it runs beside the desktop app, or an `npm start` of another checkout, which use 8787 (`src/server/ports.ts`); beside an `npm start` of the same checkout it refuses to start, since the two would share its data folder. When 8789 is in use (another checkout's `npm run dev`), `npm run dev` takes the first free one from 8789 to 8808 (`scripts/dev/ports.ts`: free at both loopback addresses, both wildcard ones and `HOST`); a `PORT` you set it takes as it is (0: any free one), and when that one is in use, nothing starts. Under `npm run dev` it is never passed on to agents' CLIs (`devRunVars()`), where another checkout's `npm run dev` would take it for its own |
| `RELAY_DEV_WEB_PORT` | `5173`, or the next free port | Vite's port under `npm run dev`: the first free one from 5173 to 5192, found as the API's is (free at both loopback addresses and both wildcard ones), and handed to Vite (`strictPort`: it never moves by itself), to the sign-in link the server prints, to the session cookie's name (`relay_session_<port>` when it is not 5173, so that two dev servers on `localhost` never sign each other's browser out: a cookie goes to every port) and to Harness Relay's own ports, which agents in the relay sandbox and the model endpoints never reach; never passed on to agents' CLIs. Set it to take that port as it is. `npm run login` names it too: for a dev server that took another port, give it the port its `[dev]` line said, or type the code on the sign-in page |
| `HOST` | `127.0.0.1` | Bind address |
| `RELAY_PREVIEW_DOMAIN` | unset | Dedicated preview domain for remote browsers (for example `preview.example.net`). Provide wildcard DNS, TLS and Host-preserving routing to Relay. See [remote access](remote-access.md#app-previews). |
| `RELAY_DATA_DIR` | `./data` | Where `relay.db` lives. One server holds it while it runs (`server.lock`): a second one with the same folder refuses to start. The relay sandbox keeps its sockets in it (`srt/`), so its path may be at most 70 bytes on macOS and 61 on Linux (UTF-8: a character outside ASCII takes 2 to 4) for the open agents to run below full access; the gateway's socket is there too (`gateway/relay.sock`): past 84 bytes on macOS and 88 on Linux, too long for a socket's path, it is listened on from the server's working folder, and the gateway does not start (the log says why) only when that is too long as well |
| `RELAY_DEFAULT_CWD` | process cwd | Initial default working directory (until changed in Settings) |
| `RELAY_ALLOWED_HOSTS` | — | Extra comma-separated host names accepted by the Host/Origin check, such as the machine's Tailscale name (see [Use it from your phone](remote-access.md)); in a GitHub codespace `.devcontainer/start.sh` adds its forwarded name ([Try it in GitHub Codespaces](codespaces.md)) |
| `RELAY_KEYRING` | — | `off` keeps API keys and MCP values in the database, encrypted with the key in `secrets.key`, instead of the OS credential store (e.g. for a throwaway data directory) |
| `RELAY_WEB_DIR` | `./dist/web` | Where the built UI is (the desktop app points it at its own copy) |
| `RELAY_KEYRING_SERVICE` | `harness-relay` | The name API keys are filed under in the credential store (the desktop app's own: `harness-relay-desktop`) |
| `RELAY_SECRETS_KEY_FILE` | `<data folder>/secrets.key` | Without a credential store: the file with the key the secrets in the database are encrypted with, 32 bytes in base64 or hex (`openssl rand -base64 32`), such as a systemd credential outside the data folder. It must be there: the server refuses to start without it (its own default one it makes). Agents below full access can neither read nor write it |
| `RELAY_APP_BROWSER_DIR` | — | Set by the desktop app: its window's browser folder (cookies, localStorage: its sign-in), which agents below full access may not read (`protectedReads()`) |
| `RELAY_UPDATES` | — | Read by the desktop app, not the server: `off` keeps it from asking GitHub for new releases |
| `RELAY_UPDATES_REPO` | `Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay-updates` | Read when the desktop app is built (`desktop/scripts/build.ts`), never when it runs: the GitHub repository (`owner/name`) the app downloads its updates from, the installers' copies for updates; never this repository (the versions and their checksums still come from this repository's releases). The [Release workflow](#releasing) sets it from its `UPDATES_REPO` variable |
| `RELAY_UPDATES_TOKEN` | — | Read when the desktop app is built, never when it runs: a fine-grained personal access token (`github_pat_…`, no other kind) the app downloads those copies with on GitHub's API, for a private repository. It goes into the app, where anyone with a copy can read it: let it read that repository's contents and nothing else. The Release workflow sets it from its `UPDATES_READ_TOKEN` secret |
| `RELAY_MAX_ACCOUNTS` | `10` | Accounts per CLI, the default one included (1–100) |
| `RELAY_MAX_TEAM_AGENTS` | `8` | Most members a team may have, set by hand or by the lead agent (1–32). A team the lead agent designs by itself keeps to 5, or fewer when this is lower |
| `RELAY_TURN_TIMEOUT_MIN` | `1440` (1 day) | Each agent turn's safety timeout in minutes, a turn waiting for the user's answer included (1–10080: a minute to 7 days); also how long a push service keeps a need for an offline browser, a day at least |
| `RELAY_VOICE_MODEL_BASE_URL` | Hugging Face, pinned to one commit | The http(s) folder voice input downloads its models from (`<url>/ggml-<model>.bin`), such as a mirror. Each file is still checked against its pinned size and SHA-256 |
| `RELAY_VOICE_ENGINE_BASE_URL` | whisper.cpp's release on GitHub | The http(s) folder voice input downloads its engine from on Linux and Windows (`<url>/<archive>`, the names of `ENGINE_BUILDS`), checked the same way |
| `RELAY_VOICE_DIR` | `<data folder>/voice` | Where voice input keeps its models and engine: an absolute path, or one starting with `~/`. Not in a temp folder (the server refuses to start), and edit mode is refused in a working directory that holds it or is inside it, since the engine runs outside any sandbox (so is it in the default folder). The engine's empty static folder stays in the data folder (`voice-public`). Removing voice input there removes the files Harness Relay downloads, by their names (the models, this version's engine), and the folders it made once they are empty; nothing else |
| `RELAY_BROWSER` | the first one found | The Chromium, Chrome or Edge the browser tools start (an absolute path, or one starting with `~/`), unless one is set in Settings → Apps and tools → Common apps → Browser, which comes first; without either, the system's Chrome, Chromium or Edge, then Playwright's newest Chromium (`src/server/browser/find.ts`) |
| `RELAY_MICROSOFT_LOGIN_URL` | `https://login.microsoftonline.com` | Where accounts that sign in with Microsoft do (Microsoft Entra's endpoints under it): another cloud's, such as a national one (`https://login.microsoftonline.us`), for its calendars (its mail is not supported yet), or a stand-in on this computer in the end-to-end tests. https, or plain http only to this computer |
| `RELAY_MICROSOFT_GRAPH_URL` | `https://graph.microsoft.com` | Where those accounts' calendars are reached (Microsoft Graph), the only host their tokens go to; Graph's scopes are then named by this address. https, or plain http only to this computer |
| `RELAY_MICROSOFT_IMAP` | `outlook.office365.com:993` | Where those accounts' mail is read (`host:port`), the only IMAP server their tokens for mail go to: a stand-in on this computer in the end-to-end tests. Not for a national cloud: its mail is not supported yet (the token for mail is the global cloud's Exchange Online's). TLS (STARTTLS on port 143); plain TCP only to this computer |
| `RELAY_MICROSOFT_SMTP` | `smtp.office365.com:587` | Where those accounts' mail is sent (`host:port`), the only SMTP server their tokens for mail go to: a stand-in on this computer in the end-to-end tests, not a national cloud's, as for `RELAY_MICROSOFT_IMAP`. STARTTLS on ports 587 and 25, else TLS; plain TCP only to this computer |
| `RELAY_RUN_CODE` | `off` | Settings → Labs' mode until it is changed there (`SettingsStore.labs()`): `on` starts it on **Let assistants script the tools**, the gateway's `run_code` tool: JavaScript that calls the turn's other Harness Relay tools as functions, each call decided by its tier as the turn's own, run in Harness Relay's own sandbox with no network in every permission ([Architecture → Harness Relay's own tools](architecture.md#harness-relays-own-tools-the-gateway), `src/server/runCode.ts`). Not on native Windows, or where that sandbox cannot start. `off` or unset: off, as before. Any other value keeps the server from starting |
| `RELAY_RLM` | `off` | Settings → Labs' mode until it is changed there: a sub-model as `<backend>/<model>`, one of Settings → Model connections (`anthropic/claude-haiku-5-5`, `homelab/Qwen/Qwen3-30B-A3B`), starts it on **Read long texts in parts**: `run_code` with `state`, kept as JSON for the rest of the agent's turn, and `llm_query()`, which the server asks that sub-model itself, with no tools, its key sent only to that connection's host (`src/server/llmQuery.ts`); or through Claude Code, Copilot or Muse Code as one of its accounts, `cli:<provider>[:<account>][/<model>]` (`cli:claude/haiku`, `cli:copilot:<account id>`), each question a toolless turn of that CLI (`src/server/cliSubModel.ts`). Whether the connection or account is set up is found out at each question. With `RELAY_RUN_CODE=off`, or a value that is not a model (`on`, `cli:codex/…`), the server does not start |
| `RELAY_CHAT_TITLES` | `on` | Whether the lead agent names new conversations after their first message, until it is changed in Settings → Lead agent (`SettingsStore.chatTitles`, `AutoGrouper` in `src/server/grouping.ts`). `off` starts it off; any other value than `on` or `off` keeps the server from starting. The unit tests (`test/setup.ts`) and the end-to-end tests' servers (`e2e/support/relay.ts`) set it to `off`, and the tests of naming turn it on |
| `RELAY_RENDER_API_URL` | `https://api.render.com/v1` | Where publishing an app reaches Render's API, for a stand-in on this computer in the end-to-end tests only (`127.0.0.1`, `localhost` or `[::1]`): the Render key goes there |
| `RELAY_NEON_API_URL` | `https://console.neon.tech/api/v2` | The same for Neon's API: a stand-in on this computer only |
| `RELAY_PUBLISH_GIT_DIR` | GitHub | A folder of bare repositories (`<folder>/<owner>/<name>.git`) git pushes a published app to instead of GitHub, for the end-to-end tests (the fake `gh` makes them there): an absolute path, or one starting with `~/` |
| `RELAY_HEADLESS_WINDOWS` | `off` | `on` for the end-to-end tests only: a stand-in for a computer that shows windows. A hand-over's window can open wherever the server runs, and opens headless, so a test runs them without a display (`e2e/specs/handover.spec.ts`). Any other value than `on` or `off` keeps the server from starting |

The server passes its own environment to the CLIs, minus Claude Code's nesting variables, the ports `npm run dev` set for its server and Vite (`PORT` and `RELAY_DEV_WEB_PORT`, `devRunVars()`) and other model providers' keys, plus stored API keys (`SettingsStore.env()`). The keys are `MODEL_KEY_VARS` in `src/server/agentEnv.ts` (the keys and tokens of `INHERITED_BACKEND_VARS`, and each CLI's sign-in by key; never `GH_TOKEN`, `GITHUB_TOKEN`, `AWS_*` but Bedrock's `AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK`, or an address, which `MODEL_ADDRESS_VARS` lists): a CLI with a sign-in keeps its own (`OWN_KEY_VARS`; Codex also those its account's `config.toml` names, `env_key`, `codexHomeKeys()`, a plain file only) and the names the user passes on to it (`SettingsStore.passEnv()`, `app_settings` key `pass_env`, `{ [provider]: names }`, set with `PUT /api/settings/providers/:provider`'s `passEnv`), and loses the others, for every one of its accounts (`withheldEnv()`). Settings shows the names only (`ProviderSettingsView.environment`: the one variable the default account signs in with, the first set in its CLI's `SIGN_IN_ORDER` unless a key saved in Settings comes first, `envSignIn()`; the keys withheld; the names passed on), and New chat says once in each browser which keys an installed CLI is not given (`src/web/agentEnv.ts`, `components/WithheldEnvDialog.tsx`). Cursor also gets `AGENT_CLI_CREDENTIAL_STORE=memory` when it runs with a key or as an account besides the default one (`CURSOR_SHARED_SIGN_IN`: what it signs in with stays in that run, never over the computer user's one Cursor sign-in), and each Cursor run gets `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR` (its private config folder) and `NO_OPEN_BROWSER=1`. Antigravity has no variable for its home, so an account's is Harness Relay's own (`HARNESS_RELAY_ANTIGRAVITY_HOME`, the folder its `.gemini` is in, never passed on to agy): `agy acp` gets `--gemini-dir <home>/.gemini`, and the commands that only ask it something (`agy models`, `agy -p /skills`…) get that folder as `HOME` (`USERPROFILE` on Windows), with `SSH_CONNECTION` set so that agy opens no browser should it find no sign-in it can use (`antigravityProbeEnv()`); the agent's own commands keep the user's `HOME`. Every Antigravity account runs on a `GEMINI_API_KEY` only: an account besides the default one gets none from the server's environment (`CREDENTIAL_ENV`), only its own key, and no account gets `AGY_ADC_AUTH` or an LLM gateway's `AGY_LLM_GATEWAY_*` and `AGY_ACP_ENABLE_GATEWAY_AUTH` (`antigravityOtherSignIn()`, in any letter case); its runs and questions tell agy to use the key (`modelProvider: "gemini"`), and without one agy is not started, so it never runs on a Google sign-in agy keeps. Each Copilot run gets `COPILOT_HOME` set to a home of the run's own (`prepareCopilotRun`, in Harness Relay's temporary folder), never `COPILOT_ALLOW_ALL` or `COPILOT_ASSISTED_APPROVAL`, and on an endpoint Copilot's BYOK variables (`COPILOT_PROVIDER_BASE_URL`, `COPILOT_PROVIDER_TYPE=openai`, `COPILOT_PROVIDER_WIRE_API=completions`, `COPILOT_MODEL`, and `COPILOT_PROVIDER_API_KEY` when the endpoint has a key) in place of any the server inherited; a Copilot account besides the default one gets none of `COPILOT_GITHUB_TOKEN`, `GH_TOKEN` and `GITHUB_TOKEN` from the server's environment, only its own token. Muse Code has no variable for its home either, only the XDG ones, so an account's is Harness Relay's own too (`HARNESS_RELAY_MUSE_HOME`, the folder of its `config` and `data` folders, never passed on to muse): the commands that only ask it something (`muse usage`, `muse login`, `muse logout`) get those as `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` and `XDG_DATA_HOME` (`museProbeEnv()`); a run, and `muse skills list --json`, get the account's `data` folder as `XDG_DATA_HOME`, a config folder of their own as `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` and `MUSE_DISABLE_APPROVAL_JUDGE=1` (`museEnv()`), which reach the commands Muse runs too. A Muse Code account besides the default one gets no `META_API_KEY` from the server's environment (`CREDENTIAL_ENV`), only its own key, and `HARNESS_RELAY_MUSE_KEY_ONLY=1` (not passed on to muse either), so that its runs never link the default account's Meta sign-in. An open agent gets it without the model keys and base URLs the server inherited (Codex's `CODEX_API_KEY` among them), nor Cursor's `CURSOR_API_KEY` and `CURSOR_AUTH_TOKEN`, nor Antigravity's `AGY_ADC_AUTH`, nor Copilot's `COPILOT_GITHUB_TOKEN` and BYOK address and keys, nor Muse Code's `META_API_KEY` (`INHERITED_BACKEND_VARS`), with the key of its own run's backend alone and its home (`HARNESS_RELAY_AGENT_HOME`) instead, and below full access only inside the relay sandbox, which sets the proxy variables and its own `TMPDIR`.

### The desktop app

`desktop/` is the desktop app ([user guide](user-guide.md#desktop-app); its files, commands and checks at a glance in [`desktop/README.md`](../desktop/README.md)). It is a package of its own, with its own `package.json` and lockfile, so `npm install` at the root never downloads Electron (about 100 MB) for people who use `npm start`. Install it once with `npm install --prefix desktop`; it uses the root's `tsx` and `typescript`, so install the root first. Its code is plain TypeScript checked by `npm run desktop:typecheck`. The parts without Electron (`env.ts`, `supervisor.ts`, `src/server/tailscale.ts`, `autostart.ts`, `prefs.ts`, `profile.ts`, `log.ts`, `update.ts`, `updateSource.ts`, `traymenu.ts`, `selftest.ts`, `i18n.ts`, `icons.ts`, `manifest.ts`, `lifecycle.ts`, and `scripts/packaged.ts`) are tested from the root `test/` like the rest; only `main.ts` imports `electron`.

How it runs:

- **The server** is `src/server/index.ts`, bundled by esbuild into one ES module (`desktop/build/server/server.mjs`). It runs in an Electron utility process: Electron's own Node (Electron 45: Node 24, with `node:sqlite`), so the app needs no Node.js (except for Harness Relay's own sandbox: see below). The built UI (`server/web`, given as `RELAY_WEB_DIR`) and the keyring module (`server/node_modules/@napi-rs/`, native code, so it is copied, not bundled) sit next to it. In the installed app they are in the app's resources (`Resources/server` on macOS), outside the app archive, so `protect.ts`'s `RELAY_ROOT` is the app itself (`Harness Relay.app/Contents` on macOS, the install folder on Windows).
- **Messages** (`DesktopToServer` and `ServerToDesktop` in `src/shared/types.ts`, through `process.parentPort`; see `src/server/parent.ts`). The server says when it listens, or why it cannot. The app asks for a sign-in link (a new one-time code, one minute: `CODE_TTL_MS.desktop`) each time it opens the chat, how many conversations have agents at work (`busy`: `Orchestrator.busy()`, which counts runs and runs being set up, changes being made to a conversation such as a parallel reply applied, the lead agent changing a team or sorting a conversation into a group, and branches being made, with `CliInstaller.running()`, the CLIs being installed from Settings; an update waits for none), and for a shutdown, which runs the same shutdown as SIGTERM: agents are stopped and their process groups killed first. Windows has no signal to send, so this is how it stops there. A server the app started prints no sign-in code: its output goes to the app's log, which agents may read. It also tells the sign-in page so (`createApp({ desktopApp })`: `GET /api/auth` adds `desktop: true`, and the 401 message changes), which then points at the app's menu instead of a terminal: at **Open Harness Relay** in the app's window (Electron names itself in the user agent), at **Open in browser** in a browser (`src/web/signin.ts`). The chat in the app's window tells the server its language as it opens and at each pick (`PUT /api/desktop/lang`, sent only where `isAppWindow()`, and taken only from this computer's own address), and the server passes it on as a `lang` message: the supervisor hands it to `main.ts` as a request, like the chat's asks about updates, and `followLang()` keeps it in `desktop.json` (`withLang()` in `prefs.ts`: only `zh-TW` or `en`, only a new one), switches the app's messages, rebuilds the tray menu and the menu bar, and rewrites the Linux autostart entry. At a start the app's messages are in the kept language, else the system's (`appLang()` in `i18n.ts`). Started by `npm start` or `npm run dev`, the server has no parent and none of this happens.
- **The environment.** On macOS and Linux the app runs the user's login shell (`$SHELL -i -l -c`, printing `/usr/bin/env -0` between two marks) before it starts the server, at launch and again on **Restart server…** and **Start server**, and gives the server that environment, as a terminal would have it. On Windows it takes its own environment at launch; a restart or a start reads PATH and the user's variables again with PowerShell (`windowsEnv()`, `mergeWindowsEnv()`): PATH as Windows makes it for a new program (the system's, then the user's), followed by the app's own other entries; the system's other variables are left alone (some, such as `USERNAME`, are defaults the session replaces). If that fails it adds the usual install folders to PATH instead (`withCommonPaths`). `serverEnv()` then sets `RELAY_DATA_DIR` (the app's data folder, unless the user set one), `RELAY_KEYRING_SERVICE` (`harness-relay-desktop`, with the app's own data folder, which also leaves out a shell's `RELAY_SECRETS_KEY_FILE`, the key file of another data folder's database), `RELAY_DEFAULT_CWD` (the home folder, unless set), `RELAY_WEB_DIR`, `RELAY_APP_BROWSER_DIR` (the window's browser folder, below), `PORT` (from `desktop.json`), `HOST=127.0.0.1`, and `RELAY_ALLOWED_HOSTS` with this computer's tailnet name: as Tailscale reports it (also before it has connected, when it knows it), else the last one seen (`tailnetName` in `desktop.json`), so that a start at login before Tailscale connects still lets the phone in. It drops `ELECTRON_*` variables.
- **The supervisor** (`supervisor.ts`) checks that the port is free, starts the server, waits for it to listen (three minutes at most: after an update macOS may ask for the keychain first), and keeps the end of its output for the error dialog. It asks for sign-in links and how many conversations have agents at work (each in order; no answer after 5 s). On a stop it asks the server to shut down and kills it if it has not exited in 20 seconds. A stop while the server starts kills it at once instead: it has not listened, so it runs no agent, and quitting while macOS asks for the keychain does not wait for the three minutes. Each server process has its own queues of questions, so one that exits late cannot cancel the next one's. An exit nobody asked for, after the server ran, is reported as a crash.
- **Quitting** (from the menu, on SIGINT or SIGTERM, or when macOS or Linux shuts down) waits for the server to stop before the app exits.
- **The window** shows the chat: a `BrowserWindow` (sandboxed, context isolation, no Node) that loads a sign-in link from the server, so it is signed in at once (as a browser of its own). `guardContents()` (`lifecycle.ts`) keeps its pages on the server, for new windows, navigations and redirects: `linkAction()` sends other web pages and mail to the default browser, opens the server's own pages (files, attachments, which need the window's sign-in) in windows of the app, and goes nowhere for anything else (`file:`, `javascript:`). `permissionAllowed()` lets the server's pages use the microphone (audio only, for voice input), write to the clipboard, go full screen (a video's own button) and show notifications (Settings → Notifications and sound), and nothing else; Electron's permission check for media without a kind counts as one for the microphone (`permissionChecked()`). Its browser data (cookies, localStorage: its sign-in) is in `browser/` of the user-data folder (`profile.ts`, Electron's `sessionData`), which the server is told as `RELAY_APP_BROWSER_DIR` and `protectedReads()` adds to what agents below full access may not read; the rest of the user-data folder holds the data folder, which parallel mode's worktrees are in, so it cannot be kept from them as a whole. A sign-in an earlier build kept in the user-data folder itself is removed at start. **Open Harness Relay** on a window that shows the sign-in page (it has `data-signin`) loads it again with a new link. Downloads use Electron's save dialog. The window's size is kept in `desktop.json`. Closing it leaves the app in the tray (`window-all-closed` is handled); on macOS the Dock icon shows while a window is open (the app is `LSUIElement`). The app menu (Edit, View, Window, in the user's language) gives the window the usual shortcuts, copy and paste included, which macOS only routes through a menu; on Windows and Linux it shows with Alt. **Open in browser** in the tray menu opens the same in the default browser.
- **The tray menu** is rebuilt on every state change, always with the same items (by id), shown or hidden. On macOS and Windows an open menu keeps showing what it showed when it opened: changing an item, or setting a new menu, changes nothing on screen until it opens again (tried with Electron 45 on macOS). So while it is open (`menu-will-show` to `menu-will-close`, and a moment after, since the click on an item comes after the menu closed), its items are changed in place (`patchMenu()`, `traymenu.ts`) and no new menu is set, and `LiveMenu` closes it and opens it again (`closeContextMenu()`, `popUpContextMenu()`; on Windows where the user opened it) when what it shows changed, such as an update's progress, at most once a second.
