It opens it again 50 ms after closing it (`REOPEN_DELAY_MS`), outside the closing menu's own loop: opened from inside it, each menu runs inside the one before on macOS, and after a few the app takes seconds to quit, or never does. The old menu's close is expected before or after the new one says it opened. Linux's menus (D-Bus) are set anew as before. Opening the app again (`second-instance` on Windows and Linux, `activate` on macOS, the Dock icon) shows the window (`reopenAction()`). Open at login uses Electron's login items on macOS and Windows, and an XDG autostart entry on Linux (`autostart.ts`), started with `--hidden` (macOS reports `wasOpenedAtLogin` instead) so that it does not open the window. On Linux, an autostart entry that is on is written again at each start, so that it starts the AppImage the app runs from.
- **Sharing with the tailnet** runs `tailscale serve --bg`, on HTTPS 443 unless another service, or Funnel, uses that port (then 8443 or 10000: `sharePlan()`, `freeHttpsPort()`; sharing a funneled port for the tailnet would turn Funnel off). When `tailscale serve status --json` cannot be read, nothing is shared.
- **The log** (`log.ts`) is the server's output: started over, the previous one kept as `.old`, past 5 MB, at a start and while the server runs. A log that cannot be written is given up on quietly, never an uncaught error.
- **Updates** (`update.ts`). About 30 seconds after the first start, then every hour (`CHECK_EVERY_MS`; at most every five minutes when the chat asks, `mayAskGitHub()`), a packaged app (unless `RELAY_UPDATES=off`) asks `GET /repos/…/releases?per_page=20` when it is a release candidate or a build of your own (`releases/latest` would leave out pre-releases), and `GET /repos/…/releases/latest` when it is a release (`LATEST_RELEASE_API`: found however many release candidates came out since); `checkNow()` says whether a check asks at all, and a question is given up on after 30 s (`CHECK_TIMEOUT_MS`). `parseRelease()` takes a release that is not a draft and whose tag is a version of ours, a release candidate too, with each system's installer by the endings of `site/assets/downloads.js` (`INSTALLERS`, `isInstallerUrl()`), fetched from its copy for updates (`updateCopyUrl()`: `-update` before the extension, `UPDATE_INSTALLERS`, under the same tag in the repository `UPDATES_REPO`, or the one the build named, `RELAY_UPDATES_REPO`, `updateSource.ts`, so that GitHub's download counts tell updates of an installed app from new installs and the release page lists the installers alone; still named as the installer, which the AppImage takes; the installer itself as its `fallback`), a plain file name and GitHub's `digest` (SHA-256) of the installer, the copy being the same file; `newestRelease()` takes the newest by version (`compareSemver()`), not by when it was published, and leaves release candidates out for an app that is a release (`takesCandidates()`: an app whose semver has no `-rc` or `-dev`). When it is newer (`compareSemver()` against `app.getVersion()`) and has this system's installer, the tray menu offers it, and so does the chat: `main.ts` sends the server an `update` message (`DesktopUpdate`: a newer version, an update under way, the last check) each time that changes and once the server listens (`Supervisor.tell()`), the server keeps it (`src/server/updates.ts`, `Updates`) for `GET /api/update`, and passes the chat's asks back (`update-check` with an id, `update-install`, which the supervisor emits as `request`); the app answers each check by its id (`answered`, `CheckAnswers`) in the first state it tells once no check is under way, so a state already on its way never ends a newer check. `installPlan()` says how this copy updates. An app built with the download mirror (`MIRROR`, below in Releasing; `main.ts` passes it as `mirror`) downloads the installer from there first (`downloadUpdate()`, `mirrorAsset()`: under the copy's name, `mirrorCopyUrl()`, which the mirror serves from the installer's file and never counts), in parts as below, checked against the same SHA-256 with nothing to fall back to; only once it has the whole file is GitHub asked as a download from it asks (`countUpdate()`: the copy located, with the token at GitHub's API alone for a private repository, the installer itself when the copy is not there, then the first byte at the CDN it redirects to, without the token), so that the update is counted against the copy as before, once. Any failure on the mirror (not reached, a 404 or 503 for a release it does not have, a release candidate say, a minute without a byte, the wrong file) and the download is made from GitHub as without a mirror, counted there by itself; GitHub not answering the count within 30 s leaves the update uncounted, and downloaded. `download()` writes the installer into `.part` (from its `fallback` when GitHub answers 404 for the copy: a release published before the copies moved, or their repository gone, or private while the app has no token for it) and checks its SHA-256, and gives up after a minute without a byte (`DOWNLOAD_STALL_MS`), so a dropped connection does not hold the update and every check after it. It comes in parts, on up to six connections at once (`DOWNLOAD_CONNECTIONS`), as on some networks GitHub's CDN sends each connection only so fast (some 200 KB/s from Taiwan) and several together go several times faster: GitHub's address is asked once where it leads, without following it (`Locate`; `main.ts` uses `net.request` with `redirect: 'manual'`, as `net.fetch` keeps nothing of a redirect), so that GitHub counts the download once, and the parts are fetched with `Range` from the signed address it sends (good for up to an hour). `main.ts` gives each connection an in-memory session of its own (`updateConnections()`): Chromium sends one session's requests to a host down one HTTP/2 connection, which goes no faster than one request. A connection done with its part takes the second half of what the part with the most left still has, while that is at least twice `MIN_PART_BYTES` (1 MB); a part that fails, or goes a minute without a byte, is asked for again from where it stopped, at a new signed address when its own has expired (403, located once however many parts find it), up to three failures in all (`PART_FAILURES`). A file under 2 MB comes in one request from GitHub's address, and so does one from a server that sends the whole file for a part (from where GitHub sent it). `updateSystem()` picks the installer: the Apple Silicon one on macOS (none on an Intel Mac), the running app's processor's on Windows and Linux (`process.arch`: an x64 app emulated on Windows on Arm stays x64). Once the new version is downloaded and checked, `whenIdle()` waits until the server says no conversation has agents at work (`Supervisor.busy()` every 2 s; one that does not answer is asked again), the menu saying *Update ready: switches once replies finish*, or until the user chooses **Switch to the new version now…** (after a dialog). `busyWithoutAsking()` says what the app knows without asking: a server that stopped by itself (a crash) runs nothing, but one that starts or stops (a restart from the menu) gives no answer yet, and once the app quits (also as the system shuts down) there is never one, so quitting never switches. The running app is not touched until then: after the wait, an app that is quitting puts nothing in place and keeps this version, and the next start removes what was downloaded. On macOS `stageMacApp()` mounts the `.dmg` (`hdiutil`), copies the app next to the running one (`ditto`) and checks it (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`, its bundle id and version) before the wait, and after it `swapMacApp()` renames the two while the server still runs, so that a failed swap changes nothing; a shell started with `spawnDetached()` (`src/server/process.ts`) waits for the app to exit and `open`s the new one. Its script is fixed text: the app's pid and the new version's path reach it in its environment (`HARNESS_RELAY_RELAUNCH_PID`, `HARNESS_RELAY_RELAUNCH_TARGET`, removed before the new version starts), never in its text or its arguments. On Windows the installer runs with `--updated /S --force-run` once the app has quit. On Linux the new AppImage downloads hidden next to the running one (`appImageDownload()`; one left by a quit during the wait is removed at start, `removeAppImageLeftovers()`), is put in place after the wait (`appImageTarget()`, `placeAppImage()`), the autostart entry follows, and it is started once the app has exited. A copy that cannot update itself (`not-packaged`, `no-installer`, `no-checksum`, `read-only`, `not-appimage`) gets the release's page. The confirm dialog's **What’s changed**, and `available.changes` for the chat's card and dialog (`chatUpdate()`), are `changesPage()`: GitHub's comparison from the running version (`__RELAY_VERSION__`) to the new one (`COMPARE_PAGE`, `compare/<running>...<new>`), which lists every change since it however many versions came out in between (a release's own notes start from the release before it, `previousVersion()`); the release's page when the running version is no release's tag (a build of your own) or is not older.
- **Updates with a token** (`update.ts`), for an app built with `RELAY_UPDATES_TOKEN`, which a private copies' repository needs (built in, it goes the same way for a public one). `copyToDownload()` first asks GitHub's API for the release under that tag there with the token (`GET /repos/…/releases/tags/<tag>`, given up on after 30 s, `CHECK_TIMEOUT_MS`, through `lookupSignal`, the installer itself then downloaded), and the copy is downloaded from its address on the API, by its id (`/releases/assets/<id>`), with `downloadHeaders()` (the token, `Accept: application/octet-stream`); where the copy cannot be had (no such release or file; the token expired or revoked, the requests every app shares run out, GitHub's API failing, or any other answer than the file or a redirect to it: 401, 403, 404, 429, 5xx and the rest; no answer at all, also none within 30 s, `answerMs`, as a stalled download is given up on only after a minute, failing the update), the installer itself is downloaded (`unavailable()`, `locateWithToken()`, `answeredWithin()`), also when asked again for an expired signed address halfway through (the parts left then come from the installer, the same file; a part started meanwhile goes on to the copy's address, with the token) and after a server that sends the whole file for a part, so a token gone bad only counts those updates as new installs. Without a token, of GitHub's answers only a 404 sends it to the installer: any other fails the update, which then offers the release's page. A copy that fails its checksum (replaced in its repository, or broken on the way), with or without a token, is fetched once more, from the installer itself and without the token; not when all the bytes came from the installer already (a download that went on from it halfway through is fetched again whole). `main.ts` goes through `downloadUpdate()`, which looks the copy up and downloads it, so that an asset and its headers always go together. The token goes to GitHub's API alone: `download()` refuses headers for an address anywhere else, and asks the copy's address where it leads with them (`Locate` takes the headers, also for a new signed address), the CDN it sends to and the fallback never get it, a request that still carries it (the API answering with the file itself) follows no redirect (`redirect: 'error'`), and without `locate` the fallback is fetched instead. A check never sends it: the releases it reads are this repository's.
- **Its own check.** Started with `--smoke-test[=<report>]`, the app checks itself and exits with 0 or 1, without the single-instance lock and with a temporary user-data folder, so it runs beside a Harness Relay in use: the keyring module loads, the server starts in a utility process (port 0, `RELAY_KEYRING=off`), passes the checks of `selftest.ts`, says no conversation has agents at work, a hidden sandboxed window signs in with a link and shows the chat, and the server stops. With `--smoke-test-clis=<folder>`, the fake CLIs of the end-to-end tests in that folder answer a question first (`checkTurn()`, below): they come first on the server's PATH, with a home folder, CLI homes and a working directory of their own in the temporary folder (`withFakeClis()`), so the user's own CLIs are never started or read. `desktop/scripts/check-package.ts` runs it, always with the fakes.

Building: `npm run desktop` and `npm run desktop:dist` build the UI (`npm run build`), then run `desktop/scripts/build.ts`, which writes `desktop/build/`:

- `app/` holds what Electron runs: `main.cjs`, its `package.json` (from `src/manifest.ts`; `productName` names the user-data folder `Harness Relay`) and the tray icons, and the repository's `LICENSE`, whose terms that `package.json`'s `license` names and which must go with every copy (`electron-builder.yml` also puts it in the app's resources, where people can open it).
- `server/` holds the server, the UI and the keyring module, and what runs apart from the server: Harness Relay's own sandbox host (`relaySandboxHost.mjs`, bundled from `src/server/relaySandboxHost.ts` with the sandbox runtime left out), Pi's gate extension (`piGate.js`, which Pi loads by its path), and the sandbox runtime's packages in `node_modules/` (`@anthropic-ai/sandbox-runtime` and what it needs; its Linux seccomp helpers are native programs).
- `resources/icon.png` is the app icon, for electron-builder.

`RELAY_VERSION` (set by the [Release workflow](#releasing)) builds the app as a release's version: its semver in `package.json` (see `semverOf()` in `scripts/release.ts`), the version itself in the menu, the log and a problem report (the server's bundle gets it as `__RELAY_VERSION__`, read by `src/server/about.ts`). Without it, the app is a build of the day in Taiwan, with `-dev` and the commit (`devVersion()`): `2026-11-10-dev (30f3d4f)`, as semver `2026.1110.0-dev`, which sorts before every release and release candidate of that day, so that one of that day or later is offered as an update.

`RELAY_UPDATES_REPO` (`owner/name`) names the repository the app downloads its updates from (the installers' copies; the versions and checksums are always this repository's releases), `UPDATES_REPO` (`Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay-updates`) when it is not set, and `RELAY_UPDATES_TOKEN` a token the app downloads them with on GitHub's API, for a private one (`updateSourceFrom()` in `desktop/src/updateSource.ts`, each trimmed: the build stops on a repository that is not `owner/name` or is this one, and on a token that is not a fine-grained personal access token, `github_pat_` and then letters, digits and `_` (a classic or OAuth token cannot be kept to one repository), and never prints the token). Both go into `main.cjs` alone (not the server's bundle) as `__RELAY_UPDATE_SOURCE__`, fixed for that build: nothing on the computer the app runs on points it elsewhere. The token is encoded there (`encodeUpdateSource()`: base64, reversed; read back by `decodeUpdateSource()` in `main.ts`), so that it never stands in the app as a token's text, which GitHub revokes once it finds one pushed to a public repository or gist; the encoding hides it from no one. The build also writes `build/update-source.txt`, a SHA-256 of the two that names neither (`updateSourceFingerprint()`), which the Release workflow checks against its own. Anyone with a copy of the app can read the token, so let it read that one repository's contents and nothing else; the [Release workflow](#releasing) sets both.

`npm run desktop` then starts Electron on `build/app`. `npm run desktop:dist` runs `desktop/scripts/dist.ts`, which runs electron-builder (`desktop/electron-builder.yml`) and ends by naming the installer and the app to double-click: the installer for the system it runs on (`.dmg`, NSIS `.exe`, `.AppImage`) in `desktop/release/`, and the app itself next to it (`desktop/release/mac-arm64/Harness Relay.app`, say). `npm run desktop:dist -- --output <folder>` puts them elsewhere, to try a build without replacing an app in use. It refuses to start while a Harness Relay runs from the output folder (macOS and Linux, from `ps`; on Windows a running app keeps its files locked anyway), and at the end says when one runs from elsewhere: opening the new build would bring the running one forward instead. Building for another system or processor is not set up: build on each (the Release workflow builds the Arm64 installers on GitHub's Arm runners).

`npx tsx desktop/scripts/smoke.ts` runs the bundled server the way the app does, with plain Node standing in for Electron (`desktop/scripts/parent-port.mjs` gives it a `process.parentPort` over Node's IPC): the keyring module must load from next to the server, the sandbox host and Pi's gate must be next to it and the host must find the sandbox runtime, and the server must listen on 127.0.0.1, serve the UI, refuse the API until a browser signs in, give a sign-in link whose code works, have a team of claude, codex and grok answer a question, pass the language a signed-in browser sends (`PUT /api/desktop/lang`) on to the app, print no code, say that no conversation has agents at work, and exit with code 0 when asked to shut down. The team are the end-to-end tests' fake CLIs (`e2e/fake-cli/`, built by `installFakeClis()` from `e2e/support/fake-cli.ts`: scripts on macOS and Linux, Node single executables on Windows): `checkTurn()` in `selftest.ts` starts a read-only roundtable chat of the three, sends it a question and waits for the run to end, and each one's reply must be done and repeat the question, so the server found each CLI by name, spoke its protocol (stream-json, `codex app-server`, ACP) and streamed the reply into the conversation. It never runs the real CLIs. The developer's own `RELAY_*` and `E2E_*` variables are left out of the server's environment (`withoutOwnSettings()` in `selftest.ts`, which the app's own `--smoke-test` and `check-package.ts` use too), and `withFakeClis()` leaves out the user's credentials (API keys and tokens), so a real CLI found instead of a fake has none. CI runs it after building the desktop app, the Release workflow on each system, and `test/parent.test.ts` runs the same checks on `src/server/index.ts`.

`npx tsx desktop/scripts/check-package.ts [--dir <folder>]` checks the packaged app electron-builder left next to the installer (`desktop/release/` by default: `mac-universal/` or `mac-arm64/`, `win-unpacked/` or `win-arm64-unpacked/`, `linux-unpacked/` or `linux-arm64-unpacked/`): its fuses as `electron-builder.yml` sets them (read back with `@electron/fuses`), the server, the web app and the keyring module in its resources (both processors' native parts in a universal Mac app), the sandbox host, Pi's gate and the sandbox runtime (on Linux, its seccomp helper executable), the repository's `LICENSE` in its resources and in its archive (`app.asar`, read with `@electron/asar`), no GitHub token (`github_pat_` and the rest of one) in the files the server runs (`server.mjs`, `relaySandboxHost.mjs`, `piGate.js`: the one for updates is the app's main process's alone) nor as text in the app's `main.cjs` (only encoded), the repository and the token its own environment names (`RELAY_UPDATES_REPO`, `RELAY_UPDATES_TOKEN`; the default repository without them), encoded, in `main.cjs` (it cannot tell what the app was built with, only compare it with these, so run it with the build's values: CI packages the app with a made-up token for this, and the Release workflow gives both the same), and the app's own `--smoke-test`, given the fake CLIs (built first, as for `smoke.ts`) with `--smoke-test-clis`, so the packaged app also has its agents answer a question. The app gets this script's environment without `RELAY_*` and `E2E_*`. On Linux it needs a display (`xvfb-run`) and runs the app with `--no-sandbox`. The Release workflow runs it on each system after making the installer, and CI on a Linux and a Windows package (`electron-builder --linux dir`, `--win dir`).

A few things to know:

- **electron-builder leaves out a `node_modules` folder** at the top of a folder it copies, whatever the filter says. So the keyring module is copied as an entry of its own in `extraResources`.
- **Fuses.** The packaged app turns off Electron's run-as-Node mode and `NODE_OPTIONS` / `--inspect` (`electronFuses`). Otherwise any program could run code as the app and use the privacy permissions macOS gave it. This is also why the shell's environment is read with `env -0` and not by running the app as Node, and why Harness Relay's own sandbox host, a Node program run for each sandboxed open-agent turn, runs in the app with the Node.js 22.12 or later found on the login shell's `PATH` (`hostNode()` in `relaySandbox.ts`): without one, the open agents are refused below full access with a message saying so (`relaySandboxNoNode`), and full access still works. It loads its code only from its archive, and checks the archive (`enableEmbeddedAsarIntegrityValidation`, on macOS and Windows), and `file:` pages get no extra privileges (`grantFileProtocolExtraPrivileges: false`; the window shows only the server's pages). Cookie encryption stays off: the window's sign-in is in a folder agents below full access may not read, its key is in localStorage, which it would not encrypt, and on an app signed ad hoc macOS would ask for the keychain after every update. `check-package.ts` reads all of them back.
- **Signing.** On macOS the app is signed ad hoc (`mac.identity: '-'`, and `dist.ts` turns off electron-builder's search of the keychain), on every computer the same: never with a certificate electron-builder would otherwise find in your keychain. Its entitlements (`desktop/entitlements.mac.plist`) are electron-builder's defaults plus the microphone, which the hardened runtime refuses otherwise; Info.plist says why it asks (`NSMicrophoneUsageDescription`, translated in `desktop/macos/*.lproj/InfoPlist.strings`). Apple Silicon runs no unsigned code, and the fuses change the Electron binary after it was linked: an unsigned build is killed at launch (exit code 137). The ad-hoc signature is not trusted by Gatekeeper, so a downloaded app needs **Open Anyway**, and macOS asks again for the keychain after each update. Signing with a Developer ID and notarizing, and signing for Windows, are not set up yet. Updates are the app's own (`update.ts`), not electron-updater's, and rest on GitHub's checksums over HTTPS.
- **The Mac app is for Apple Silicon only** in a release (`--mac dmg --arm64`, the macOS runner's own processor): there is no app for Intel Macs, which run Harness Relay from the source. The website counts a Mac as one with Apple Silicon unless Chromium's hints say Intel (Safari and Firefox say "Intel" on every Mac); the app offers no update on an Intel Mac. The Mac apps of 2026-11-11 and its release candidate were universal and look for `-mac-universal.dmg`, so they do not find the releases after them. `npm run desktop:dist` builds for the Mac it runs on.
- **Windows and Linux have an installer per processor** in a release: x64 (`win-x64.exe`, `linux-x86_64.AppImage`) and Arm64 (`win-arm64.exe`, `linux-arm64.AppImage`). The Arm64 ones are built on GitHub's Arm runners (`windows-11-arm`, `ubuntu-24.04-arm`, free for public repositories), not cross-built, so that npm installs the keyring module's native part for Arm and the packaged app's own check runs there. The Windows Arm64 job builds on a Dev Drive of its own (a copy of the checkout, npm's cache and the temporary folder): the runner's one disk writes small files about 14 times slower than a Dev Drive does, which made `npm ci` take two minutes there.
- **Linux AppImages** may need `--no-sandbox` where unprivileged user namespaces are restricted (Ubuntu 24.04). electron-builder's `executableArgs` only reach a `.desktop` file, not an AppImage started directly; the app passes the flag on to its autostart entry when it was started with it.
- **To try it apart from your own data**, run the built app with a user-data folder of its own and without opening its window, and give that folder a `desktop.json` with another port if a Harness Relay already listens on 8787: `desktop/node_modules/.bin/electron desktop/build/app --user-data-dir="$HOME/relay-desktop-try" --hidden` (not under `/tmp`: the server warns about a data folder in a temporary folder, which sandboxes let agents write in). The server's log is `~/Library/Logs/Harness Relay/server.log` on macOS, and `logs/server.log` in the user-data folder on Windows and Linux.

## Request flow

1. The client posts `POST /api/conversations/:id/messages` → `Orchestrator.send()` prepares the workspace (for edit / full access: `git init` if needed, and the directory lock unless the mode is parallel; while another conversation holds it, the team's own worktrees instead), appends the user message and starts `execute()` in the background (the request returns `202`).
2. In an auto-team conversation, a run for a new question only lets the lead agent design the team (`designTeam()`, see [Architecture → Auto team](architecture.md#auto-team)); the team answers in a run of its own once the design is confirmed. Otherwise `execute()` chooses speakers by mode (creating per-agent worktrees first in parallel mode) and calls `turn()` for each.
3. `turn()` creates a streaming agent message, records the starting state (worktree `HEAD` or a snapshot), computes the messages the agent hasn't seen, builds the prompt (`prompt.ts`), and calls `runners[provider].run()` in the right directory, passing `requestApproval` unless the conversation refuses automatically.
4. The adapter spawns the CLI (`process.ts`; an open agent's through `startOpenProcess()`, inside the relay sandbox below full access), parses each stdout line and emits `AgentEvent`s.
5. `turn()` applies events to the message (text, thinking, plan, tool rows and output), emits SSE events through the `Hub`, and saves through the `Store`. An approval request adds an approval part and waits for `POST …/approvals/:approvalId`. When the CLI exits it records the files changed (commit in the worktree, or a second snapshot) on the message.

## HTTP API

All write requests must send `Content-Type: application/json` (CSRF guard). Errors are `{ "error": string }` with status 400/404/409/415/500. Send `X-Relay-Lang: zh-TW | en` to get errors, system notes and built-in templates in that language (the web app always does).

Every request but `GET /api/auth`, `POST /api/auth/login`, `POST /api/oauth/callback` (which takes a sign-in's state instead) and `POST /api/hooks/:id` (which takes the schedule's own token instead) must come from a signed-in browser ([Architecture → Signing in](architecture.md#signing-in)): the `relay_session` cookie and the `X-Relay-Key` header. The routes the browser loads by itself (`BROWSER_LOADS` in `app.ts`: replies' media and downloads, attachments, `files/raw`, `files/download`, `files/zip`) take the cookie alone, and `…/events` takes the key as `?key=`. Otherwise the answer is 401 with `code: "signed-out"`. Tests that are not about signing in pass `auth: null` to `createApp()`.

| Method & path | Body → response |
| --- | --- |
| `GET /api/auth` | → `{ signedIn, desktop? }`: whether the request comes from a signed-in browser (always true without sign-in); `desktop: true` when the desktop app started the server (the sign-in page then points at its menu) |
| `POST /api/auth/login` | `{ code }` → `{ key }`, and sets the `relay_session` cookie (`relay_session_<port>` under an `npm run dev` whose Vite took another port than 5173; replacing the session of a browser signed in already); 400 for a wrong or expired code, 429 after ten wrong codes in a minute |
| `POST /api/auth/codes` | → `{ code, expiresAt, phoneUrl? }`: a one-time code for another device, working for ten minutes. The server supplies its current private HTTPS `phoneUrl` when sharing is configured |
| `GET /api/auth/sessions` | → `AuthSessionView[]`: the signed-in browsers, the latest used first, `current` for the one asking. A mobile session has `phone: "online"` while a page is in front, otherwise `"paired"` |
| `DELETE /api/auth/sessions/:id` | signs that browser out (and clears this one's cookie when it is this one); 404 for an unknown one |
| `GET /api/conversations` | → `ConversationSummary[]` (with `running` (a run under way, also while it is set up), `waiting`, `lastStatus`, `lastReplyId`, `groupId`, `pinnedAt`, `sorting`, `createdAt` and `branchFrom` for the sidebar). `?page=<id>`: the page asking (its own id, up to 64 letters, digits, `-` and `_`), with `&visible=1` while it is in front, so that the server holds its browser's push notifications, and without it when it is not (a tab in the background, a page that came back), so that the server sends what it held ([Architecture → Notifications](architecture.md#notifications)) |
| `GET /api/push` | → `PushStatus`: `{ publicKey }`, the key a browser subscribes to push notifications with (VAPID, base64url; made the first time it is asked), or `{ error }` when the server cannot send them (its key could not be saved in the database) |
| `PUT /api/push/subscription` | `{ subscription: { endpoint, keys: { p256dh, auth } }, prefs: { done, needs, sound } }` → `{ ok }`: keeps this browser's push subscription, for its signed-in browser and in the request's language (updating its own one of the same `endpoint`). 409 with `code: "push-taken"` when another signed-in browser has that subscription, and with `code: "push-gone"` when its push service said it is gone (either way the browser subscribes anew); a browser keeps its latest five; 400 for an address that is not a known push service's over HTTPS (`PUSH_HOSTS` in `src/server/push.ts`), keys that are not a P-256 public key and a 16-byte secret, or preferences that are not booleans; 503 when the server cannot send push notifications |
| `DELETE /api/push/subscription` | `{ endpoint }` → `{ ok }`: removes this browser's subscription (only its own) |
| `POST /api/push/test` | `{ endpoint }` → `{ ok }`: sends this browser a test notification through its push service; 404 when that subscription is not this browser's, 502 when the push service did not take it (a subscription it says is gone is removed) |
| `POST /api/push/presence` | `{ visible, page }` → `{ ok }`: this browser's page (`page`: its id) went to the background (`false`: the push notifications held for the browser are sent now, unless another of its pages is still in front) |
| `GET /api/notifications` | → `InboxItem[]`: the notification inbox (the sidebar's bell), the newest first: each alert the team gave (`kind`, with the `agent` it is about and what it said as one short line, `text`), its conversation (`conversationId`, and its `title` now), when (`createdAt`) and whether it was `read`; the same for every signed-in browser |
| `GET /api/notifications/summary` | → `InboxSummary`: `{ unread, unreadIn, newest? }`, how many are unread, the conversations with unread ones (the latest first) and the newest notification's id; the page asks it with each poll of the conversations |
| `POST /api/notifications/read` | `{ ids }` (at most 500, each 1–64 characters), `{ conversationId }` or `{ all: true }` → `{ read }`: marks those notifications, that conversation's or all of them read, and says how many were unread; 400 for anything else (`validateInboxRead()`) |
| `DELETE /api/notifications` | → `{ ok }`: removes every notification |
| `GET /api/search?q=` | → `SearchResult[]`: conversations whose title, user / agent messages or ready lead reviews (what they say, not their JSON) contain every word of `q` (ignoring case), title matches first, then the most recent; at most 50. A branch's result has `branchFrom`, the title of the conversation it was branched from |
| `GET /api/cwds` | → `WorkingDirectories`: `{ home, dirs: [{ path, default? }] }`, the working directories the working directories box offers: the default ones (`default: true`, `~` expanded as a new conversation reads them) first, then the conversations' own (all of each), the most recently used first; each folder once (ignoring case on macOS and Windows), and only folders that still exist. `home` lets a typed `~` match. `pick: true` when the browser can open the folder dialog: it asks by a loopback name (`localhost`, `127.0.0.1`, `[::1]`, never through `tailscale serve`) and this computer has one |
| `POST /api/cwds/pick` | `{ start? }` → `PickedFolders`: `{ paths }`, the folders chosen in this computer's own folder dialog (several on macOS and with zenity; one with kdialog and on Windows), none when it was cancelled. It opens at `start` (`~` expanded), or the nearest folder above it that exists, or the default working directory when `start` is empty, and is answered when the dialog closes (closed when the request is, or after ten minutes). 403 when not asked by a loopback name, 503 when this computer has no dialog (Linux without zenity or kdialog, or without a graphical session), 409 while one is open already, 502 when it could not be opened |
| `PUT /api/conversations/:id/group` | `{ groupId }` → `{ ok }`: puts the conversation in a sidebar group (`null` = Other), and with it the rest of its family of branched conversations; 404 for an unknown group. A conversation the user moved is never sorted automatically |
| `PUT /api/conversations/:id/pin` | `{ pinned }` → `{ ok }`: pins the conversation to the top of the sidebar (`true`) or unpins it (`false`); pinning one that is pinned already keeps when it was pinned. Neither is activity: its `updatedAt` stays. 400 unless `pinned` is a boolean. The conversation list carries `pinnedAt` |
| `GET /api/groups` | → `ConversationGroup[]`, in the order they were made (the default group, Other, is not listed) |
| `POST /api/groups` | `{ name }` → `ConversationGroup` (400 for an empty, too long or taken name — including Other / 其他 — or more than 50 groups) |
| `PATCH /api/groups/:id` | `{ name }` → `ConversationGroup` |
| `DELETE /api/groups/:id` | deletes the group; its conversations go to Other |
| `POST /api/groups/suggest` | `{ request? }` (what the user wants, if they say: at most 4000 characters) → `GroupSuggestion` (`create`: new groups, each with a `key`; `rename`: `{ id, from, name }`; `move`: `{ convId, title, from, to }`, `to` a group's id, a new group's key or `null` for Other; `reason`, `meta`): the lead agent reads the groups and one conversation for each family of branches (its oldest, the 300 most recently used families) and suggests how to organize them, read-only, in an empty folder of its own; only suggestions that can be taken come back (a taken name, a number that names nothing, a move that changes nothing, and a new group nothing moves into are left out); nothing changes (400 with no conversation yet or for a request too long; 503 when the lead's CLI fails or its reply is unusable; 409 when it was stopped: the request closed, or a newer one came) |
| `POST /api/groups/reorganize` | `{ create?: [{ key, name }], rename?: [{ id, name }], move?: [{ convId, to }] }` (`to`: a group's id, a `key` of `create`, or `null` for Other) → `{ groups, created }`: applies the suggestions the user took, together, in one transaction: each move takes the conversation's whole family, as the user's choice (the lead agent does not sort it again); refused whole, changing nothing, for a taken or invalid name, a bad key or more than 50 groups (400), or a group or conversation that is not there (404) |
| `POST /api/conversations` | `{ title?, agents, settings, createCwd?, firstMessage?, attachments?, requestId? }` → `Conversation`; with `settings.autoTeam: true`, `agents` is ignored (empty) — the lead agent designs the team for each new question, and `settings.autoConfirm: true` uses its team without asking; `settings.leadReview: true` has the lead agent review the team's replies once it has answered (any conversation; off without it, while the New chat and New automation pages send it ticked by default); `settings.steer: true` sends the user's messages into the running turns of the members speaking, where their CLI can take one (off without it); `settings.extraCwds`: the working directories after `settings.cwd` (at most eight in all, each once; without `settings.cwd`, the default ones); working directories that do not exist are a 404 with `code: "cwd-missing"` and `paths` (those missing) unless `createCwd: true`, which creates them. Optional `firstMessage` and `attachments` go through the normal message flow together. Attachments use the same format as a chat message and require a nonblank question. A refused send leaves no new chat |
| `GET /api/conversations/:id` | → `Conversation` |
| `PATCH /api/conversations/:id` | `{ title?, agents?, settings?, createCwd? }` → `Conversation` (409 while running; neither `agents` nor `settings` switches auto team on or off, `settings.autoConfirm` can change; `settings` without `leadReview` turns lead review off, except that a switch on the design card made while the request is read and its workspace prepared still stands; setting `agents` confirms a team design that waits, and that team then answers; the design the team comes from (that one, else the latest one used, passing over designs declined since) then has the team's `agents` and `settings` (mode, rounds, hops) and `adjustedAt`, and loses its `savedPresetId`, unless the team is as it has it; `settings` alone does the same to the latest design used, never to one that waits; `settings` without a `cwd` keeps the conversation's working directories, and a `cwd` without `extraCwds` makes it the only one; missing working directories as in `POST`) |
| `DELETE /api/conversations/:id` | stops a run and deletes |
| `PUT /api/conversations/:id/schedule` | `{ text, repeat, timeZone?, enabled?, approvals?, missed?, newChat?, goal?, command?, attachments?, removeAttachments?, fromProposal? }` → `ConversationSchedule`: sets the conversation's schedule (`command`: a fixed command of its working directory, by id, run first each time its own times come: 400 for another folder's) (one per conversation; replaces the one there was, keeping how it went last time). `fromProposal`: the id of the reply whose proposed schedule the form saves, whose card then says it was created (404 when it proposed none, 409 when it was dealt with already). `repeat` is `{ kind: 'once', at }`, `{ kind: 'daily', time }`, `{ kind: 'weekly', time, days }` (0 = Sunday), `{ kind: 'interval', hours }` (1–168, from when its times were last set; saving only a new message or other choices keeps the cadence), `{ kind: 'manual' }` (no set times) or `{ kind: 'event', triggers }` (when something happens: 1 to 5 triggers, any of which starts it, never two for the same source (the same account and folder, calendar, repository, what is watched there and the branch of CI runs, or pattern); an older client's single `trigger` is taken as the only one, and the answer has `triggers`; each trigger is one of: `{ source: 'mail', account, folder?, from?, subject? }`, a mail account from Settings by its id, the inbox when no folder is given (`INBOX` in any letter case is the inbox, answered as `INBOX`; any other folder keeps its case); `{ source: 'calendar', account, minutes, title? }`, a calendar from Settings by its id, a whole number of minutes from 1 to 1440 before each event; `{ source: 'github', repo, on: 'pr' \| 'ci', branch? }`, `owner/name` or its address on github.com (an owner of at most 39 characters, a name of at most 100), a branch for `ci` only, a name git allows (no spaces, none of `~^:?*[\`, no `..`, not starting with `-`); `{ source: 'file', pattern }`, relative to the working directory, without `..`, a drive or a leading `/`, `\` read as `/` and a leading `./` dropped (so `./` alone is empty); each text a line of at most 200 characters, its conditions (`from`, `subject`, `title`) trimmed and left out when empty; 400 for any of these, and, when it is saved on, for a mail account or calendar that any of them names that is not there, when it is on; what it kept of each trigger's source stays while that trigger stays (the same source, wherever it is in the list) and the automation stays on, and goes with a trigger removed; the answer's `watches`, one for each trigger checked, leave out the events and files it saw); `time` is `HH:MM` in `timeZone` (default: the server's). Defaults: on, `approvals: 'deny'`, `missed: 'skip'`, `newChat: false` (`true`: each time in a new conversation with the same team, settings (lead review included) and group). 400 for an empty or too long message, a bad time, no days, a one-time schedule in the past (when on) or an unknown time zone; 409 when it is on and the team is still to be designed or confirmed Files use the same uploads as chat messages. Each save adds `attachments` and removes the saved IDs in `removeAttachments`. Omit both to keep the files. Repeating an upload with the same name, MIME type and contents keeps a single copy. The response includes metadata only. |
| `POST /api/schedules` | `{ title?, settings, createCwd?, schedule, hook? }` → `Conversation` (with `newHook`, `{ token, url, hook, platform }` as `POST …/schedule/hook` answers, when `hook` is `true`: **Create a webhook too**): a new schedule (**New automation**) in a conversation of its own, with auto team (`settings` as in `POST /api/conversations`; `schedule` as in `PUT …/schedule`, on by default; the title defaults to the message's first line). The lead agent starts designing the team for the schedule's message at once (`designForSchedule`); nothing is posted, and once the team is confirmed nothing runs until the schedule sends the message. The schedule is checked before the working directory is created; 400 for a schedule that could never send, or one that waits for a mail account or calendar that is not there (on or off), 404 (`code: CWD_MISSING`) for a missing working directory without `createCwd` A schedule with a fixed command is refused (400: a new chat has none of its own). |
| `POST /api/schedules/draft` | `ScheduleDraftRequest` (`{ request, schedule?, timeZone, convId? }`: the form as it stands, the browser's time zone, the drawer's conversation) → `ScheduleRevision` (`schedule`, a `ScheduleUpdate`; `reason`, `meta`): the lead agent writes the automation form's schedule as asked, read-only, in an empty folder of its own, told the time in that zone, the conversation's team (none: a new automation) and the mail accounts and calendars set up (ids and names only). A once time it gives as the user's wall clock (`YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM`) is made an instant in that zone; a trigger keeps an account only when it is set up (the only one there is when it named none), else `''`, for the user to pick in the form; `enabled` stays as the form had it; checked as a saved schedule is (`validateSchedule()`). Nothing is saved (404 for an unknown conversation; 400 for an empty or too long request; 503 when the lead's CLI fails or its schedule cannot be one, such as a time that has passed; 409 when stopped) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/messages/:messageId/schedule` | `{ action: 'create' \| 'dismiss', timeZone? }` → `ConversationSchedule` (`create`) or `{ ok }`: the card of a schedule the reply proposed (its `schedule` part, `ScheduleProposal`): `create` saves it as `PUT /api/conversations/:id/schedule` does (on, approvals refused, in `timeZone`, the browser's; it replaces the conversation's schedule; the fixed command it names, by the id of the command of that name saved for the working directory, and `commandOnly`), `dismiss` sets it aside; either way the part's `status` says so (`created`, `dismissed`). 404 when the reply proposed none, 409 when it was dealt with already or the command it names is not saved, 400 for another action or a schedule that cannot be saved as it is (a time that passed, a trigger with no account: the form takes it then) |
| `DELETE /api/conversations/:id/schedule` | → `{ ok }`: removes the schedule |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/schedule/design` | → `202` `{ ok }`: has the lead agent design the team for the schedule's message (again, e.g. after a design was stopped), as `POST /api/schedules` does. 404 without a schedule; 400 when the conversation has no auto team; 409 when it has a team already, while it runs, or while a design waits for confirmation |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/schedule/run` | → `202` `{ ok, queued, ran?, noted?, chatId? }`: sends the schedule's message now, as when it comes due (it waits in the queue while the team replies; `chatId`: the new conversation it opened, for a schedule with `newChat`; `ran`: its fixed command found nothing new, so nothing was sent; `noted`: a schedule that only runs its command found something, which a note in the chat says); its times stay as they are. 404 without a schedule; 409 with the reason when it is held back (the team waits for an answer, is not confirmed, or an earlier scheduled message is still waiting), the conversation is busy (a settings change, an apply) or it cannot start (another conversation writing in the same folder holds nothing up: the team then works in its own worktree) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/schedule/check` | `{}` → `ConversationSchedule` (or `null` when it was removed meanwhile): checks everything an automation that starts when something happens waits for, now, each trigger on its own, and sends its message when something new is there (always asking the source, also while new mail is held for the team); while the automation is off it only records what is there, as the first check does, and so does a check of files while a run writes in the working directory, or the first one after it. `409` for a schedule of another kind, `503` for a server without a scheduler |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/schedule/hook` | `{}` → `{ token, url, hook, platform }` (`platform`: the server's system, for the example's shell): a webhook for the schedule (a new token replaces the one it had); the token is returned this once, and only its SHA-256 is kept. `409` without a schedule 409 for an automation that only runs its fixed command (`commandOnly`), which takes no webhook. |
| `DELETE /api/conversations/:id/schedule/hook` | → `{ ok }`: removes the schedule's webhook |
| `POST /api/hooks/:id` | `Authorization: Bearer <token>`, a JSON body of at most 20 KB → `202` `{ ok, queued, chatId? }`: a program on this computer sends the schedule's message now, the body with it as `event`. The one route without a browser's sign-in: a missing or wrong token, or no schedule, is a `404`; `413` for a larger body, `429` within 10 seconds of the last call, `409` when the conversation cannot take it (with the reason) or its tracked goal is reached and the schedule is off 409 for an automation that only runs its fixed command (`commandOnly`), even with the right token. |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/messages` | `{ text, attachments?, interrupt?, requestId? }` (empty text and no files = continue) → `202` `{ ok, queued }`. While the team replies (or its design waits for confirmation) the message waits in the queue (`queued: true`) and is delivered before the next turn; `interrupt: true` stops the team and sends it now. While the lead agent reviews the team's replies, a message (text or files) always does, as with `interrupt` (when another conversation took the folder meanwhile, the team then works in its own worktree); continue is refused (409) then. `attachments`: `[{ name, mimeType, data }]` with base64 `data`; at most 10 files of 20 MB each |
| `DELETE /api/conversations/:id/queue/:messageId` | → `ChatMessage`: withdraws a message still waiting to be delivered (404 once it was delivered) |
| `GET /api/conversations/:id/attachments/:attachmentId` | → the attached file (images inline, anything else as a download) |
| `GET /api/conversations/:id/messages/:messageId/media?path=` | → an image or video the reply embeds, from the working directory, the agent's worktree or the attachments (`nosniff`, sandboxed, byte ranges, ETag; anything else is a 404) |
| `GET /api/conversations/:id/messages/:messageId/download?path=` | → a file the reply links to, from the same folders (never `.git`), always as a download (`application/octet-stream`, `attachment`, `nosniff`, sandboxed, byte ranges with `If-Range`, ETag; HEAD sends no body) |
| `PUT /api/conversations/:id/messages/:messageId` | `{ text }` → `202`: edit a user message — it and everything after it are deleted, every agent starts a fresh session, and the team answers again (`Orchestrator.resend()`) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/messages/:messageId/retry` | → `202`: delete an agent reply and what follows, and let that agent (and the later speakers of its round) answer again |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/messages/:messageId/branch` | → the new conversation: a copy of the messages up to and including an agent reply, with the team, settings, "always allow" answers and sidebar group, which goes on from there on its own (`Orchestrator.branch()`; 400 for a message that is not an agent's reply, 409 while a message before it is still streaming) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/approvals/:approvalId` | `{ allow, always?, note? }`: answer an agent's approval request (404 when already answered or expired) |
| `PUT /api/conversations/:id/messages/:messageId/steps/:index` | `{ done }` → `{ ok }`: ticks (or unticks) a step of a reply's steps card while the team waits for the user, kept and sent to every browser (`Orchestrator.tickStep()`; 400 for a step that is not there, 404 for a message with no steps card, 409 once it is answered or no longer waited for) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/messages/:messageId/steps/answer` | `{ done: number[], stuckAt?, note? }` → `202` `{ ok, queued }`: answers a steps card: the steps done (by index), the one the user is stuck at, a note of at most 2,000 characters. The card keeps the answer, and the answer is posted as the user's message in the request's language (`answerSteps()`, `stepsAnswerText()`), so the team goes on with the agent that asked first (alone, when others wait too). 400, 404 and 409 as for a tick, and 409 while the team works or the lead agent reviews (the answer is never queued nor sent into running turns); a message that cannot be posted leaves the card as it was |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/messages/:messageId/secrets/:partId` | `{ values: { NAME: value } }` or `{ decline: true }` → `{ ok }`: a secrets card's answer (`answerSecrets()`): the values given (only names the card still waits for) are kept in the credential store for the card's working directory, the asks marked `saved` or `declined`, and the answer posted as the user's message, naming the secrets, never a value. 400 for a bad name or value or none given, 404 for an unknown card, 409 once answered, 503 with no credential store |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/messages/:messageId/commands/:partId` | `{ action: 'code', edits? }` → `{ diff, files, missing, elsewhere, digest }` (`files`: the same code, its files apart, `CodeViewFile`, which the card lists; `elsewhere`: what it pins that is not the command's own, which the card counts): the code a proposed fixed command would run (the scripts it names, what changed since the conversation's agents began, its git settings and hooks; a file of secrets by its names), masked, and its digest; `{ action: 'save', edits?, digest }` → `FixedCommand`: saves it (with the card's edits: `name`, `run`, `hosts`, `secrets`, `cwd`, checked again), pinned to its folder's code, only when that is still the code whose digest it sends (the card asks for the code first when the user saves with it closed); `{ action: 'dismiss' }` tells the team it was not saved; `{ action: 'continue' \| 'share' }` posts the user's answer, `share` with its last run's masked output. 400 for an invalid command, action or digest, 404 for an unknown card, 409 once decided, while it runs, without the digest or when the code changed since, for a card an automation's run proposed, for a folder that holds Harness Relay, and while the conversation cannot take the answer (busy, its queue full) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/commands/:commandId/try` | `{ extra?, stdin?, confirm? }` → 202 `{ ok }`: runs a fixed command of the chat's own working directory once, in the background (its card follows it): `extra`, words added to its command line for this run; `stdin`, what the user typed in the card's input box, given on its standard input (at most 16 KB); `confirm: true`, the user confirmed trying a command marked `publishes`. 400 for a `confirm` that is not a boolean or a `stdin` that is not text or too long, 404 for a command of another folder, 409 while it runs, or for a command marked `publishes` without `confirm` |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/commands/:commandId/approve` | `{ digest }` → `{ ok }`: approves what changed in a fixed command's code since it was pinned: the code whose diff had that `digest` (`GET …/diff`) is pinned again, only when it is still that code, and nothing runs; the conversation's cards that waited on it say so (its outbox runs, its notes, its cards' `changed` runs). 400 for a `digest` that is not one, 404 for a command of another folder, 409 while it runs, while a run writes in its folder, or when its code changed again since the diff |
| `GET /api/conversations/:id/commands/:commandId/diff` | → `{ changed, diff, files, missing, digest }`: the pinned files and links that changed since a fixed command was pinned, git's diff of them (secrets masked), those it has no diff for (ignored, outside the repository, git's own), and the digest of the code it shows, which Approve sends back. 409 when its code cannot be pinned now (a link leading out of the folder) |
| `GET /api/conversations/:id/commands` | → `FixedCommand[]`: the fixed commands of the chat's working directory (the automation's form offers them) |
| `GET /api/handoffs` | → `{ storage, folders: FolderHandoffs[] }`: Settings → Secrets and commands: by working directory, the secrets' names (with when each was set) and the fixed commands; never a value |
| `PUT /api/handoffs/secrets` | `{ folder, name, value }` → `{ ok }`: replaces a secret of a folder Settings lists (404 for another folder, 400 for a bad name or value) |
| `POST /api/handoffs/secrets/delete` | `{ folder, name }` → `{ ok }`: removes one |
| `DELETE /api/handoffs/commands/:commandId` | → `{ ok }`: removes a fixed command and its pinned snapshot, and from the schedules that ran it first (404 when there is none) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/handovers/:handoverId` | `{ action: 'start', mode: 'window' \| 'screencast' }`, or `{ action: 'done' \| 'cancel', note? }`: the user's answer to a hand-over of the chat's browser (`browser_hand_over`): start it in a mode it offers (400 otherwise), decline it, hand it back or cancel it; only the first answer of each step counts (409 for a later one or one out of step, 404 once it is over) |
| `GET /api/conversations/:id/handovers/:handoverId/screen` | A screencast hand-over's page as SSE (`HandoverScreenEvent`: `frame`, the latest only for a browser that falls behind; `location`; `notice`; `ended`), for any signed-in browser, read with fetch (the key in its header); 409 for a hand-over not shown as a screencast, 404 once it is over |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/handovers/:handoverId/input` | `{ events }`: what the user did in the screencast's panel (`HandoverInput`, at most 100: mouse, wheel, key, text, navigate, history, viewport; positions as fractions of the frame), given to the page in order (400 for one out of range, 409 before it started, 404 once it is over) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/outbox/:itemId` | `{ action: 'send' | 'discard' }` → `OutboxItem`: answers an outbox card. Confirming an outgoing message, Google Docs change or Obsidian note change posts `send`. The server applies the approved action once. `404` for an unknown item, `409` for one already answered, or for a fixed command's run while a run writes in its folder (the item still waits) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/messages/:messageId/interactive/:partId` | An interactive command's card ([Architecture → Interactive commands](architecture.md#interactive-commands)): `{ action: 'start', workdir?, seen? }` → `InteractiveSession` (in its own empty folder, or with `workdir: true` in the working directory, `seen` the configuration files the card listed there: `409` when others appeared, the card's list updated; `409` for a card an automation's run proposed, on native Windows, a dismissed card or one running; `429` past four at once); `{ action: 'input', text, hidden? }` (one line, sent as typed; `hidden`: masked wherever it shows); `{ action: 'answer', askId, text? }` (the password helper's question, refused without `text`); `{ action: 'cancel' }`; `{ action: 'send' }` (its masked output posted as the user's answer, once a run ended, once); `{ action: 'dismiss' }` (Don't run). `404` for another chat's card, `409` for typing into or answering a run that ended |
| `GET /api/connectors` | → `ConnectorsView`: the mail accounts and calendars of the mail and calendar tools, passwords masked |
| `PUT /api/connectors/mail/:id` | `{ name, address, username, password?, imap, smtp }` → `ConnectorsView`: adds or replaces a mail account (`id` a slug; a new one needs its password; without one, the kept one stays). Servers: TLS or STARTTLS, plain only to this computer, never Harness Relay's own ports. `409` for a mail account reached through an account that signs in with OAuth, whose servers and sign-in are fixed |
| `POST /api/connectors/calendar` | Adds a discovered calendar using `{ name, url, username, address?, password }`. Returns the existing collection for the same login and URL. For a new collection, Relay chooses an unused ID and display name. Returns `ConnectorsView`. |
| `PUT /api/connectors/calendar/:id` | `{ name, url, username, address?, password? }` → `ConnectorsView`: adds or replaces a calendar (https; http only to this computer). `409` for a calendar reached through an account that signs in with OAuth, which never takes a password |
| `DELETE /api/connectors/:kind/:id` | → `ConnectorsView`: removes a mail account (`mail`) or calendar, and its password (one reached through an account that signs in is added again from it) |
| `POST /api/connectors/:kind/:id/test` | → `{ ok, message? , error? }`: signs in as the saved account (IMAP and SMTP, or a calendar query; through its OAuth account for one reached so: XOAUTH2 to its provider's mail servers), sending nothing; an account that needs a new sign-in is `ok: false` with why |
| `POST /api/connectors/calendar/discover` | `{ url, username, password?, id? }` → `{ ok, calendars?, error? }`: the calendars of an account from its server's address (the password typed, or the kept one of `id`) |
| `GET /api/oauth` | → `OAuthView` `{ clients, accounts, secretStorage }`: each provider's app (its client id, whether it is Harness Relay's own, Microsoft's tenant, Google's client secret masked, whether it is disabled, whether it can sign in on another device) and the accounts that sign in with OAuth (address, uses, `connected` or `reauth` with why); never a token |
| `PUT /api/oauth/clients/:provider` | `{ clientId, tenant?, secret? }` → `OAuthView`: the app `microsoft` or `google` is signed in to with (an empty `clientId`: none of the user's own; without `secret`, the kept one stays). Another app than before (another client id or tenant, or none; a tenant compared without case, none being `common`) marks the provider's accounts `reauth`, since their tokens were the app's before, and ends its sign-ins still waiting. `503` when the credential store refuses the client secret (nothing changed), `400` for a client id, tenant or secret that is not one, `404` for another provider |
| `PATCH /api/oauth/clients/:provider` | `{ disabled: boolean }` → `OAuthView`. Pauses or resumes a provider’s direct OAuth connections. Pausing cancels its pending sign-ins and reconciles mail watchers. Its registration, accounts and grants are preserved. |
| `POST /api/oauth/signins` | `{ provider, uses?, account?, origin?, device? }` → `OAuthSignInView` `{ id, provider, status, expiresAt, authorizeUrl?, redirectUri?, device? }`: starts a sign-in for its uses (`calendar` by default, `mail`, Google’s `drive` or `documents`, or Microsoft’s `teams`; one or more uses supported by the provider): the provider's page to open, sending the browser back to `<origin>/oauth/callback` when `origin` is on this computer (else to `http://localhost:<port>/oauth/callback`), or with `device: true` (Microsoft) a code to type at an address. `account`: an account's id, signing it in again or for another use: its uses are asked for too, and its address is the page's `login_hint` (`404` for no such account, `400` for one of another provider or what is not an id); another account signed in on the page, or the account removed meanwhile, fails the sign-in and changes nothing. `400` without an app for the provider, `429` with 10 waiting. A sign-in that leaves an earlier use of the account without its access fails (Microsoft: each such use's token asked for with the new refresh token; Google: its scopes in the answer), keeping the account as it was |
| `GET /api/oauth/signins/:id` | → `OAuthSignInView`: how the sign-in goes (`waiting`, `done` with its `account`, or for an MCP server's its `server`, `failed` with its `error`), for the browser session that started it only (`404` for another) |
| `POST /api/oauth/signins/:id/address` | `{ address }` → `OAuthSignInView`: the address the provider's page ended at, pasted (a browser on another computer): its code and state finish that sign-in. `400` for what is not an address (or longer than 8000 characters), or one of another sign-in |
| `DELETE /api/oauth/signins/:id` | → `{ ok }`: stops waiting for the sign-in (a device code's answer that comes back after is not kept); for the browser session that started it only (`404` for another) |
| `POST /api/oauth/signins/:id/confirm` | `{}` → `OAuthSignInView` with its `authorizeUrl`: the user confirmed where an MCP server's sign-in page is (its `confirm`: the page or token endpoint not on its authorization server's origin), shown before it opens; recorded, and only then is the page given. For the browser session that started it only (`404` for another) |
| `POST /api/oauth/callback` | `{ state, code?, error?, iss? }` → `{ status, provider, email?, server?, error? }` (`server`: the MCP server's name, for its own sign-in): what the page the provider sent the browser back to (`/oauth/callback`) got, with the issuer its redirect named (`iss`, RFC 9207: for an MCP server's sign-in, another than its own, or none when its metadata says it is always there, fails it; a sign-in not confirmed yet fails too). It takes no sign-in: the state is the proof (one sign-in's, once, for 10 minutes); a field longer than 4096 characters is not taken. A state no sign-in has is a `404`, and after 20 of those within 10 minutes a post whose state names no sign-in here is a `429`; one with a sign-in's own state always goes through |
| `GET /api/oauth/accounts/:id/calendars` | → `OAuthCalendar[]` `{ name, url, addedAs? }`: the account's calendars, each with the calendar it was added as (its own at the address a sign-in adds it as, so it is listed as added); `409` when the account needs a new sign-in (also when its refresh is refused as they are read) or is not signed in for calendars, `404` for no such account, `502` with the provider's reason when they cannot be read |
| `POST /api/oauth/accounts/:id/calendars` | `{ name, url }` → `ConnectorsView`: adds one of them to the calendar tools (an address on the provider's hosts only: `400` for another; `409` for an account not signed in for calendars) |
| `POST /api/oauth/accounts/:id/mail` | → `ConnectorsView`: adds the account's mail account to the mail tools, at its provider's own IMAP and SMTP servers, its address as sender and user name, under an id and a name not taken (`Outlook · <address> (2)`; once: one already there stays); `409` for an account not signed in for mail, `404` for no such account, `400` when the mail accounts are at their limit. Only the account's first sign-in for mail adds it by itself |
| `DELETE /api/oauth/accounts/:id/drive` | Returns `OAuthView`. Removes the Google account’s `drive` and `documents` uses from Relay while preserving its sign-in and other services. |
| `DELETE /api/oauth/accounts/:id` | → `OAuthView` with `connectors` (`ConnectorsView`) and, for Microsoft, `permissionsPages`: removes the account, its tokens (Google's revoked) and the calendars and mail accounts reached through it |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/messages/:messageId/confirm-plan` | confirms the lead agent's team design that waits: its team becomes the conversation's and answers the question it was for, with the messages that waited (409 while something else holds the conversation) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/messages/:messageId/decline-plan` | declines it: the current team stays and answers (400 for a first question, which has no team to keep; 409 as above) |
| `PUT /api/conversations/:id/lead-review` | `{ on: boolean }` → `{ leadReview }`: lead review on or off (the latest team design card's box, and Team settings' box when it is all the drawer changed while the team replies and no design waits for confirmation); at any time, also while the team replies; off stops a review under way, and the run then ends as usual (400 when `on` is not a boolean) |
| `PUT /api/conversations/:id/steer` | `{ on: boolean }` → `{ steer }`: steering on or off (Team settings' box, saved on its own when it and lead review's are all the drawer changed while the team replies and no design waits for confirmation); at any time, also while the team replies, read as each message is sent (400 when `on` is not a boolean, 404 for an unknown conversation). With it on, `POST /api/conversations/:id/messages` answers `queued: false` for a message that went into running turns (it is posted at once, with `steered`) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/checks/:messageId/recheck` | `{ confirm? }` → `ChatMessage`: team checks: checks the conversation's latest result again, from where its run started, with the list and the folders as they are now; the new result, posted after it, its commands then run in the background holding the folders (the conversation counts as running; Stop stops them); `confirm: true` runs the commands the run's changes kept from running (409 for another result, or while the team replies) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/checks/:messageId/skip` | → `ChatMessage`: goes on without the latest result passing: `skippedAt`, the failure kept; what waits in the queue goes (409 for another result or one that passed) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/checks/:messageId/fix` | `{ agentId }` → 202: asks that member to fix the latest result: a message from the user and a turn of that member alone, shown the result (400 without `agentId`; 404 for a member not in the team; 409 as above, or while the team replies) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/checks/try` | `{ command, permission? }` (`CheckCommand`, the permission Team settings shows) → `CheckOutcome`: runs one command of the list being edited on the working directories as they are, as the checks would with that permission (in the sandbox below full access), holding the folders; what is sent meanwhile goes once it is done; nothing is posted (400 for a command or permission that cannot be used; 409 with read-only permission, or while the team replies) |
| `GET /api/checks/environment` | → `{ platform, sandbox? }`: the server's system, and why Harness Relay's sandbox cannot start there when it cannot (Team settings' commands: which opening to offer, and that they cannot run below full access) |
| `POST /api/checks/draft` | `ChecksDraftRequest` (`{ request, variant: 'team' \| 'template' \| 'defaults', checks?, convId?, cwd?, extraCwds? }`) → `ChecksRevision` (`checks`, `reason`, `readIn?`, `meta`): the lead agent writes the team checks editor's list as asked. For `team` it runs read-only in the project's first working directory (the others as its added folders): the request's `cwd`/`extraCwds` when they exist (the team drawer's, saved or not, or a new chat's), else the conversation's (`readIn` says where); for a template, Settings' default, or folders that do not exist yet, in an empty folder of its own, reading no project. Its list is checked as a saved one is (`validateChecks()`), each rule apart: one it got wrong is left out and the reason says how many; a command or hook it kept keeps its id; for `defaults` the hooks stay as they were (they are set apart). Nothing runs and nothing is saved (404 for an unknown conversation; 400, 503 and 409 as for `revise-team`) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/messages/:messageId/save-plan` | `{ name? }` → `TeamPreset`: saves the lead agent's custom team design as a template |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/revise-team` | `{ request, agents, settings }` (`settings`: `mode`, `rounds`, `maxHops`, and the drawer's `permission` and `cwd` for the lead to read) → `TeamRevision` (`agents`, `settings`, `reason`, `meta`): the lead agent changes the team sent (the team drawer's) as asked, read-only; nothing is saved (404 for an unknown conversation; 400 for an empty or too long request, or an invalid team; 503 when the lead's CLI fails, its reply is unusable, or the server is shutting down; 409 when it was stopped: the request closed, a newer one came, or the conversation was deleted) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/stop` | aborts the run |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/share` | `{ html, upTo?, files? }` → `SharedPage`: puts the page the UI made (`sharePage.tsx`) on the web as a secret gist with the user's `gh` (`gh gist create`), read at `url` (Harness Relay's viewer, `site/share/#<owner>/<id>`), and records it; `upTo`: the agent reply it ends with. `files`: `ShareFileRef[]` (`{ messageId, path }`, at most 100), the links the page's placeholder links (`relay-share-file:<n>`) name: those files, found as `…/download` would serve them, go into a second secret gist first (`gh api gists --method POST` with a placeholder, its description holding the share's token, and recorded at once as an unfinished share; then git pushes the files into it, signed in by `gh`, and `gh api gists/<id>` reads back their raw URLs), then the page gist is made with each placeholder replaced by its file's raw URL; `files` and `filesGist` in the answer say how many and where. 400 for an empty page or a list of files that is not one (or a placeholder it does not cover), 413 over `SHARE_MAX_BYTES` (10 MB, with 512 bytes for each file link), more than 20 files or 100 MB of them, 409 with `code: share-file-refused` for a file that cannot go along (any `ShareFileProblem`), 502 with the reason when `gh` is missing, not signed in or fails, or git cannot push the files (nothing recorded, except a files gist left behind when neither the page nor deleting it went through: recorded with `unfinished`, its `id` and `gistUrl` the files gist's, `url` empty, and no gh `code`), with `code: gh-missing` or `gh-signed-out` for the first two (the UI shows the steps to fix them) |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/share/files` | `{ files, upTo? }` → `{ files: ShareFileInfo[] }`: the files these links of the shared messages (up to and including `upTo`) point to, each once, with the links that point to it (`refs`), its `size`, and a `problem` when it cannot go into the gist (`missing`, `outside` the chat's folders or in `.git`, `too-large` over 50 MB); found only as `…/download` would serve them, measured but not read, and nothing of what they hold is sent. 400 for a list that is not one |
| `GET /api/shares` | → `SharedPage[]`, the newest first; `conversationGone` once its conversation was deleted, `files` and `filesGist` when files went along, `unfinished` for a files gist left without its page (not while its share is still being made) |
| `DELETE /api/shares/:id` | → `{ ok }`: deletes the share's gists, the files gist first, then the page's (`gh gist delete --yes`; one GitHub cannot find either, by `gh api gists/:id`, counts as deleted, while one of another account is refused), then the record, only once both are gone. 404 for an unknown share, 409 for the files gist of a share still being made, 502 with the reason (and the same codes) when `gh` fails for either (the record stays, to be removed again) |
| `POST /api/gh/login` | `{}` → `CliLoginView`: starts gh's own sign-in for sharing (`gh auth login --web --hostname github.com`, in gh's environment for sharing, `ghEnv()`), stopping one already running; GitHub's one-time code and device page come in `codes` and `urls` as gh prints them. 409 while `GH_TOKEN` (or `GITHUB_TOKEN` outside a codespace) is set, which gh would sign in with instead |
| `POST /api/apps/:app/hosted/:provider?account=…` | Check the selected Claude or Codex account’s hosted connector for `slack`. Claude queries only the named `claude.ai` connector with `mcp get`. Codex reads its current ChatGPT account and the enabled, callable app with the matching runtime ID. Codex returns `available` for a callable app in its passive inventory. With `{verify:true}` it uses an ephemeral thread, checks the selected app’s published read-only profile tool and policy, and asks only for the current user’s profile. A valid authenticated result returns `connected`. The response contains only the status. The user connects or disconnects on the provider’s own site. |
| `GET`, `PUT`, `DELETE /api/apps/slack/posting` | Inspect a direct Slack posting connection, connect with the workspace app’s bot or user OAuth token, or remove it. GET returns the connected identity. |
| `GET /api/apps/teams` | Check for PowerShell 7 and Microsoft.Graph.Authentication, or return the current Teams sign-in state. |
| `GET /api/apps/github/install`, `GET /api/apps/browser/install` | → `AppDependencyView`. Inspect availability, install support, manual command and job status. |
| `POST /api/apps/github/install`, `POST /api/apps/browser/install` | Start or reuse a background installation for that prerequisite and return the same view. |
| `POST /api/apps/teams/install` | Prepare Teams sign-in. If PowerShell 7 is missing, download Microsoft’s portable package for the platform, verify its pinned SHA-256 hash and install it under the Relay data folder. Reuse an existing Graph authentication module if a separate PowerShell process can import it. Save a missing module from PowerShell Gallery into a staging folder, check that it imports, then publish it under `clis/teams-modules` in Relay’s data folder. Poll the status endpoint for completion. |
| `POST /api/apps/teams/signin`, `DELETE /api/apps/teams/signin` | Start the Microsoft device sign-in, or cancel it and disconnect. The connection lives in a process owned by this Relay server. |
| `POST /api/apps/teams/posting`, `DELETE /api/apps/teams/posting` | Start, cancel or disconnect the separate posting sign-in. The read session stays connected. Poll the Teams status route for the device code, error or `canPost`. |
| `GET /api/apps/availability` | Draft readiness. `permission` accepts `read-only` (default), `edit` or `full`. Omit `app` for all apps, use `local` for local checks, or name one of `mail`, `calendar`, `teams`, `slack`, `drive`, `notion`, `heptabase`, `obsidian`, `github`, `browser`. Passive checks do not read the Slack profile. |
| `POST /api/apps/availability/slack/verify` | Explicitly verifies Slack for the selected permission. When the Codex connection needs verification, it reads the signed-in user’s own Slack profile and returns readiness without profile details. |
| `POST /api/apps/:app/signin` | Starts an official Notion or Heptabase sign-in (lowercase IDs). Slack only renews an existing official MCP grant. |
| `POST /api/apps/obsidian` | Adds `{ paths: string[] }` and returns the updated tool library. Validates the whole batch before saving. Each vault must contain a real `.obsidian` directory. Duplicate paths keep their existing IDs. An empty or invalid batch, or a vault nested inside another connection, returns 400. |
| `DELETE /api/apps/obsidian/:id` | Disconnects the specified vault and returns the updated tool library. Other vaults and files on disk are preserved. An unknown ID returns 404. |
| `PUT /api/apps/obsidian` | Single-vault compatibility. With zero or one connection, `{ path }` selects or replaces the vault, and an empty string disconnects it. Returns the tool library. With multiple vaults connected, returns 409. |
| `GET /api/apps/github` | Returns `connected` and the active GitHub login, `signed-out` or `missing`. The first two can include `tokenSource`, the name of the environment variable supplying the token (`GH_TOKEN` or `GITHUB_TOKEN`). |
| `POST /api/apps/github/signout` | `{ login }` → `{ ok: true }`. Rechecks the active login and signs that account out of the local GitHub CLI on github.com. Returns 400 for an invalid login or 409 if the active account changed or an environment token supplies the sign-in. Refresh status after success to see any other active account. |
| `GET /api/gh/login` | → `CliLoginView` or `null`: that sign-in as it runs (`running`, then `success`, `failed` or `cancelled`) |
| `DELETE /api/gh/login` | → `{ ok }`: stops it |
| `GET /api/apps/docker/install` | → `PreviewDockerView`. Reports a missing CLI, an unavailable engine or a ready local Linux engine (`state`). Includes `available`, `canInstall` and, when relevant, the diagnostic, manual command and installation job. |
| `POST /api/apps/docker/install` | Starts or reuses a background Docker installation on the Relay host and returns the same view. The panel requests installation when the user chooses **Install Docker**. |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/preview/target` | `{ folder, entry?, root?, messageId?, expectedFolder? }` → `{ folder, mode, entry }`. Resolves the canonical folder and detects the preview entry and mode. Use a Files `root` or the author’s `messageId` for a reply. A changed `expectedFolder` returns 409. |
| `POST /api/conversations/:id/preview` | `AppPreviewRequest` → `{ id, url, origin, terminal?, partial? }`. `partial: true`: a static page whose folder could not be copied whole, previewed with only the files it names. Send the resolved `mode` and `entry` with the canonical directory in `expectedFolder` to bind the start to that selection. A changed directory returns 409. Starts `static`, `build`, `server` or `container` mode and returns a single-use bootstrap URL. A `container` preview (`entry`: a compose file or `Dockerfile`) answers before its build with `terminal: true`; a compose file Relay refuses is a 400 naming the key. A target with nothing to run (`…/preview/target`) answers with the code `preview-no-recipe`. |
| `DELETE /api/conversations/:id/preview/:previewId` | → `{ ok: true }`. Stops the named session and revokes its credential. |
| `GET /api/conversations/:id/preview/:previewId/terminal` | SSE of `PreviewTerminalEvent`s: the terminal so far (`output` with `replay`), the phase (`building`, `starting`, `running` with its `command`, `exited` with its `code`, `failed` with its `error`), `page` once the app serves HTTP, then live `output` (base64), and `closed`. For the browser that started the preview only (403 otherwise); its pings keep the preview from expiring as idle. |
