- `buildOrganizePrompt()` / `ORGANIZER_BRIEFING` (`grouping.ts`) — the lead agent's suggestions for the sidebar's groups: the groups with how many conversations each has, one conversation for each family by number, the user's request; new groups numbered after the ones there are.
- `buildTemplatePrompt()` / `TEMPLATE_BRIEFING` (`planner.ts`), `buildChecksPrompt()` / `CHECKS_BRIEFING` (`checksDraft.ts`), `buildSchedulePrompt()` / `SCHEDULE_BRIEFING` (`scheduleDraft.ts`) — the editors' "Ask the lead agent": the template (as the drawer's team is revised, with its name and description), the team checks (reading the project first, when there is one), and the schedule (the time in the user's zone, the accounts set up), each with its strict JSON shape (`TEMPLATE_SCHEMA`, `CHECKS_SCHEMA`, `SCHEDULE_SCHEMA`).
- `proposalRules()` (`prompt.ts`) — the briefing's line on proposing a schedule (`<schedule>{…}</schedule>`, read by `src/shared/scheduleProposal.ts`), left out of runs a schedule started; `forwardText()` gives teammates a proposed one as a `<proposed_schedule>` block of data.
- `buildReviewPrompt()` / `REVIEWER_BRIEFING` (`reviewer.ts`) — what the lead agent is asked when it reviews the team's replies (lead review): what was said since its last review, the replies numbered (`reviewMaterial()`), the conversation before it, what its last review said, the team, and in parallel mode each member's worktrees; it must return a summary, highlights (each a kind, a note, and for one reply its number and words copied from it) and next steps (the user's, or the team's with the message to send it).
- `longTermSection()`, `recalledSection()` and `memoryLine()` (`prompt.ts`) — what members are told long-term memory is for and how to save, correct and set aside a note, with the pinned notes, and the notes recalled for a turn; `buildTidyPrompt()` / `TIDY_BRIEFING` (`memory.ts`) — what the lead agent is asked when it tidies the notes, with the record of the new ones. The fakes of the end-to-end tests know the tidy prompt by the line that asks for its JSON object, `{"changes"` (`tidyReply` in `e2e/fake-cli/lib/plan.cjs`).

Prompts are in English on purpose (agents reply in the user's language). User-facing text is translated — see [Translations](#translations).

## Translations

The UI and the server's user-facing text come in Traditional Chinese (`zh-TW`) and English (`en`).

- **UI strings** — add the key to `src/web/locales/zh-TW.ts` first: its shape is the `Messages` type. `en.ts` is typed `Messages`, so TypeScript reports a missing or mistyped English entry; text with values is a function (`(n: number) => …`). In components use `const t = useT()` (or `useI18n()` for the language and `setLang`). Code outside React gets the messages passed in (`relativeTime(ts, t)`, `conversationMarkdown(conv, t)`).
- **Server strings** — `src/server/i18n.ts` has both dictionaries; call `t()` where the text is produced. The language comes from the request (`X-Relay-Lang`) through AsyncLocalStorage and follows the runs the request starts, so do not cache translated text in long-lived objects.
- **Built-in templates** — `src/server/presets.ts` keeps names, descriptions and roles in both languages.
- **Another language** — add it to `Lang` / `LANGS` in `src/shared/types.ts`, add a locale file and a server dictionary, the template texts, and its detection rule in `browserLang()`.

`test/i18n.test.ts` checks that both UI dictionaries have the same shape (including list lengths, which types don't check).

The guided tour is a checklist: its translated topics in `tour.steps` follow `TOUR_STEPS` in `src/web/tour.ts`, each with an id (its picture in `TourArt.tsx`, its button's address in `TOPIC_LINKS` in `Onboarding.tsx`) and a last-changed revision. A topic shown is read at its revision (`relay:guide` in the browser's storage, with the version a "Not now" answered); a browser that finished the tour before the checklist kept only a version (`relay:onboarded`), and every topic up to it counts as read. The tour opens by itself on a first visit only; after that, while topics are left to read, the home page asks about them (`TourNudge.tsx`) until **Not now**, which holds until a topic gets a revision past it. For a change worth reading again, bump `TOUR_VERSION` and give that revision to each new or revised topic. Keep the order in both locales and update the fingerprint in `test/tour.test.ts`.

## Common app registrations

**Settings → Apps and tools** has one navigation entry and two tabs. The existing `#/settings/apps` and `#/settings/tools` routes open **Common apps** and **Tool library** respectively. **Common apps** groups its ten rows under Mail and calendar, Team messaging, Documents and notes, and Development and browsing. All four named regions are visible on the same tab. Mail and Calendar come first and each has its own service chooser and list of connected accounts. **Choose your mail service** offers **Gmail**, **iCloud Mail**, **Outlook** and **Other mailbox**. Gmail and Google Calendar use the same Google desktop OAuth client. A configured service offers **Sign in with Google**. Service choices are always selectable once loaded. Only the selected service shows its disabled sign-in action and reason if the provider’s registration is incomplete or turned off. Its explanation links to the online setup guide and **Set up Google sign-in** opens **Settings & sign-in → Apps and tools → Common apps → Account sign-in settings (OAuth 2.0)**. The panel lists each provider’s status and can disable or enable its direct connections independently, preserving its registration and existing grants. It stores the client ID and secret for Google or the client ID and tenant for Microsoft. Existing manual mailboxes keep their saved credentials. iCloud Mail uses its preset servers. Other mailbox offers manual IMAP/SMTP settings, including Fastmail and Yahoo Mail presets. Under **Choose your calendar service**, Calendar offers **Google Calendar**, **iCloud Calendar**, **Outlook** and **Other (CalDAV)**. Selecting a service shows its setup controls. Under **Connected mailboxes** and **Connected calendars**, a single group per OAuth account contains all of that service’s resources. Users can remove individual resources and add them back after the last one is removed. The shared OAuth settings panel lists the accounts under each provider and handles account-wide disconnection. Manual resources keep their own entries. Apple’s email and app-specific password fields use the existing CalDAV discovery and save endpoints with the iCloud server supplied by Relay. Browser includes the browser executable setting. Seven built-in tool groups use **Choose assistants to enable this tool** on their service rows. For Slack posting, connect **Send from Relay**, then open **Assistants who can draft messages** inside that section. **Tool library** contains MCP servers, Skills, Memory, Hooks and Default team checks. Drive, Teams, Slack, Notion, Heptabase, GitHub and Obsidian work without publisher registration. Slack offers hosted connections for Claude and Codex. Each opens the partner’s authorization flow. Access is tied to the end user’s assistant account and workspace policy. [OpenAI plugins](https://openai.com/business/plugins/) · [Claude connectors](https://claude.com/marketplace/connectors-plugins)

Drive management shows each connected account’s email, granted uses and actions in its own card. Removing Drive stops that account’s Drive reading and Google Docs editing in Relay. The shared OAuth settings can disconnect the entire Google account. The first connection and **Add another Google account** use the same three options. Search and read is the default, with choices for reading and editing or editing only. Direct Drive search and reading use a user-supplied Google Cloud desktop OAuth client, the Drive API and the `drive.readonly` scope. Creating a Google Doc or editing one by ID uses the Google Docs API and explicit consent to the independent `documents` use (`https://www.googleapis.com/auth/documents`). The Drive tools `drive_create_document({ account?, title, content })` and `drive_edit_document({ account?, id, before, after, tabId? })` enqueue proposals in every permission mode. Confirming an edit applies the single approved text replacement to the selected tab at the previewed revision. The `content`, `before` and `after` arguments each accept up to 48 KiB of UTF-8. The matched passage must fall within one body paragraph and begin and end at a grapheme boundary. Confirming a create makes the document and inserts its text. If the insertion fails, the result identifies the document that Google created.

The portable installer in `src/server/connectors/teamsRuntime.ts` uses the system’s `curl` and `tar`. It supports macOS arm64 and x64, Windows arm64, x64 and x86, and Linux glibc arm64, arm32 and x64 or musl x64. It verifies the pinned archive digest and starts the extracted executable before publishing it to the data folder. An installation error with `retry: "install"` lets the user retry from the Teams row. When Relay needs to install the Graph module, it downloads `Microsoft.Graph.Authentication` 2.41.2 from the PowerShell Gallery and verifies the pinned SHA-512 digest before installing it.

The Teams row uses Microsoft Graph PowerShell’s first-party device sign-in for a work or school account. It works independently of the Microsoft OAuth client used by Outlook. The first sign-in grants read-only access. A second device sign-in on the same tenant and work or school account grants optional posting access. Graph resolves the team and channel or existing chat from the supplied Teams link and verifies the target tenant before the outbox preview and again before posting. Direct Slack posting uses the user-supplied workspace app token and validates its sender and destination at Send. The Slack row can renew an existing shared grant with its original registered client. Drive, Gmail and Google Calendar share one Google OAuth registration. Release builds ship without a default Google or Microsoft client.

Notion and Heptabase use the fixed official servers in `src/shared/apps.ts` with the existing discovery, dynamic registration, PKCE and credential store. A catalog server is given to supported assistants after a successful sign-in and keeps its identity across retries and renames. Teams uses a process-scoped Graph PowerShell token cache. On Windows the SDK shares its normal profile’s account hint, which can store the account name and tenant ID. The helper protects an existing hint during sign-in. GitHub uses the existing gh device sign-in. The tool library stores each Obsidian vault’s ID and canonical path. Every assistant given the Obsidian tool group can use every connected vault. Drive, Teams, GitHub and Obsidian lookups use the gateway’s read tier. `obsidian_list_vaults` returns `{ vaults: [{ id, path, available }] }` with an optional `nextOffset`. Pass it as `offset` to fetch the next page. `GET /api/tools` includes the full list of connected vaults. Listing folders, reading notes and proposing changes take the selected ID in `vault`. It is optional with one connected vault and required with multiple vaults. `obsidian_create` and `obsidian_edit` also take a vault-relative `path` and complete Markdown `content`. They queue a preview through the outbox in every permission mode. At confirmation the server rechecks the vault, folder and target against that preview. Coverage lives in `test/teams-graph.test.ts`, `test/oauth-mail.test.ts`, `test/mcp-oauth.test.ts`, `test/tools.test.ts`, `test/app.test.ts` and the Settings flows in `e2e/specs/oauth.spec.ts`, `e2e/specs/teams.spec.ts` and `e2e/specs/mcp-oauth.spec.ts`.

## Testing

```bash
npm test               # all tests
npx vitest test/store.test.ts
```

The tests run on macOS, Linux and Windows (CI runs them on Linux and Windows). For a test to pass on each:

- Build paths with `join()` and `resolve()`, not as text with `/`: on Windows `/repo` resolves to `C:\repo`, or to the drive of the folder it is resolved from (GitHub's runners check the repository out on `D:`, with the home folder on `C:`, and the temporary folder there too except in CI's unit tests, which put it on `D:`). A function that takes a `platform` builds that platform's paths (`posix` or `win32` from `node:path`), whichever system it runs on.
- Split a PATH with `delimiter` (`;` on Windows), and write a path into a git config file with `/`: the file reads `\` as an escape.
- Skip what only macOS or Linux have with `it.skipIf(process.platform === 'win32')` (or `describe.skipIf`): permission bits, POSIX signals and process groups, `/bin/sh`, and stand-in CLIs run by their `#!` line, which Windows cannot start.
- Leave time limits to `test/timeouts.ts` and `test/setup.ts`: `vi.waitFor` waits up to 5 s unless a test says otherwise (Vitest's own 1 s is too short for a run that records its changes with git while all the test files run at once), and on Windows, which starts processes and runs git several times slower, every test's limit and every wait is six times longer; a test that sets its own limit uses `slow(ms)`. The orchestrator's tests allow 20 s each (`test/orchestrator-harness.ts`): each runs git several times, and their files run side by side.
- Wait for what a test needs (`vi.waitFor`), not for a fixed time: Windows' timers tick every 15.6 ms.
- Windows' temporary folder may be named by its short 8.3 name (`C:\Users\RUNNER~1\…` on GitHub's runners), which `realpathSync` keeps and git and the system's own real path (`realpathSync.native`, `fs.promises.realpath`) spell out. `test/setup.ts` sets `TEMP` to the name spelled out, so the tests' folders are named as the folder dialog would name them.

`test/setup.ts` also removes every `RELAY_*` variable before a test file loads its modules, so a limit or a voice folder you set for your own Harness Relay does not change what the tests see; a test that needs one sets it itself. The end-to-end servers leave them out too (`e2e/support/relay.ts`), and a spec gives its own with `serverEnv`.

Current tests:

- `test/store.test.ts` — SQLite store and settings (round-trips, each conversation's latest agent reply in the list (which and how it ended, from memory and from the database), a tool call's kind kept and one this server does not know dropped, streaming updates, crash recovery (replies left streaming aborted, the approvals they waited on expired, their running tool calls ended), cascading deletes, file permissions), pinned conversations (when each was pinned, kept when pinned again; not activity, also after a save and a reopen; none for an unknown or deleted conversation), sidebar groups (order, renaming, deleting a group moves its conversations to Other, automatic sorting only once and never over the user's move or into a deleted group; a family of branched conversations moved as one, also once its top is deleted, a new branch in its family's group, a branch taken along when its original is sorted; a conversation deleted in the middle of a family remembered below it (`via`, in memory and in the database, an unreadable one left out), so the family stays together; where a branch's earlier copied messages were written (`earlier`, read back, an unreadable part left out, and given `via` once the conversation that wrote them is deleted), and a conversation's title read without loading it (`titleOf`), for a deleted one the title the sidebar shows; where each conversation was branched from, and when it was made, in the list), search (every word, ignoring case, title matches first, Chinese, `%` and `_`, system notes left out, the excerpt, replies still streaming, what a branch was branched from, what a ready review says found (its summary, notes, quotes and steps, words with quotes or backslashes too) but not its JSON, nor anything of a failed one), and API keys with a fake credential store: never written to the database, old database keys moved out and scrubbed from the files, a refusing store. Also what each CLI's runs leave out of its own setup (kept, and only what that CLI can leave out: hooks, subagents and plugins included), and the single items left out (kept as JSON, only the kinds that CLI can leave out alone, a copy, kept through other updates and cleared by an empty object). Also the CLIs' own homes: each CLI's home variable pointing into the folder in place of the inherited one, the folders created (and still pointed at, with a warning, when they cannot be), saved with AgentsView's addresses, and the inherited homes back when it is off. Also schedules: kept with the conversation (and the messages they sent), listed for the sidebar, the due ones most overdue first, and gone when removed or when the conversation is deleted. Also auto team and `autoConfirm` kept, and lead review (`leadReview`) and a review message (schema version 29) kept and turned off again, and steering (`steer`) and a message's `steered` members (schema version 39) kept and turned off again, and schema version 13 turning auto team on for conversations saved before it; whether a schedule opens a new chat each time and the chat it last opened kept, and schema version 14 leaving older schedules in their own conversation; a conversation's sidebar group read back (`groupOf`); the working directories in use, once each, the most recently used first (`listCwds`). Also shared conversations: kept the newest first with the reply each ends with, one whose conversation was deleted marked, and forgotten (schema version 15); how many files went along, their gist and a files gist left without its page kept, and older shares read back without them (schema version 16); a share put in the place of its files gist's record in one step; a branch saved with the messages it starts with in one step (nothing when one cannot be written), and where its copied messages end (schema version 17; an unreadable one left out). Also the data folder's lock: one holder at a time, freed when it closes or its process is killed, with no file beside it, and still held after garbage collection when nothing refers to its handle (as in index.ts); and the folders the open agents' homes have been in (`openAgentRoots`), kept across a change of the agent-home folder and given to their runs. Also accounts: each CLI's other accounts kept with their names, homes (in the CLIs' own folder, else in the data folder) and keys (in the keyring, moved there from the database; only their own: none of the credential variables in the server's environment, such as Claude Code's OAuth and auth tokens), an unknown account taken as the default one, a removed one forgotten with its key and the lead agent moved back to the default one; each account's own default model and effort and what its runs leave out (a new account with none, only what its CLI can leave out, copies handed out, an account that is gone taken as the default one), and the accounts there were before schema version 20 given their CLI's values; members', template members' and agent sessions' accounts kept. Also Cursor's one sign-in: `AGENT_CLI_CREDENTIAL_STORE=memory` for an account besides the default one, and for a run with a key (the account's, or the server's own `CURSOR_API_KEY` / `CURSOR_AUTH_TOKEN`), none for the default account without one; the server's own Cursor credentials for the default account only; other CLIs left alone. Also Muse Code's accounts: one besides the default one in a home of its own, with its own Meta API key only (never the server's `META_API_KEY`), marked to run on it alone; the default account keeps the server's key. Also the backend a member's session was made on (schema version 26), none for the CLI's own service. Several working directories: a conversation's others (`extra_cwds`), a turn's changes in each repository (`more_changes`), each agent's worktree of each repository and forgetting one, the list of working directories in use with all of each conversation's, migrating the worktrees made before version 21, and leaving out what cannot be read. A database the model backends migrated as 21–25 or, before the accounts, as 19–22 (unreleased builds) brought to today's numbering: today's 21 (and before the accounts also 19 and 20) run on it and its version raised by as many, what it holds kept; one of main's own at 21 left as it is. Also a review the server stopped during, kept as stopped with where it started reading. A schedule's goal, tracking state and webhook (its token's hash apart, never in a conversation) and a webhook's body on its message, and tracking that cannot be read; a schedule with no set times listed as such for the sidebar; a reply's tracked run kept (schema version 34); a conversation whose latest reply says its tracked goal is held up listed as `blocked`, from memory and after a restart, and no longer once a later reply says otherwise; an automation that starts when something happens: what it saw of its source and a message's event source kept (schema version 35), each source read back as itself (`mail`, `calendar`, `github`, `file`) and one this version does not know as the webhook's, the watch dropped for a schedule of another kind, and an error in it shown in the sidebar only while it is on; a reply's `notice` part (an MCP server left out of the turn) kept; an automation saved when it waited for one thing (its `trigger`, one watch object) read as waiting for that one, written back as a list with each trigger's watch and the first trigger also alone (`trigger`, which the version before reads, beside `triggers`, which is read first, and kept out of what browsers get), the new-mail triggers listed for IDLE one by one, due when any trigger is (one not checked yet due at once), what it kept of a trigger it no longer waits for (or of one twice) not read back, the inbox kept in another letter case read as `INBOX` with what its checks kept and the same inbox twice as once, and one waiting for nothing it can read not read at all; a team checks command a stopped server left running settled at the next start. Also each member's own limit (`limits`, schema version 39) in conversations and templates, only the one its CLI has; a hand-over of the browser kept with its reply, and one a stopped server left waiting or in the user's hands expired. Also a proposed schedule's part (`schedule`: its proposal and status) read back, and one kept in a shape this server cannot read read as one with no message; one conversation for each family of branches for the lead agent's group suggestions (its oldest, the most recently used family first, its title as it is now: `familyHeads()`), and applying the suggestions taken in one go (new groups after the others, renames, each family moved as one, as the user's choice: `reorganize()`). Also a note's card of a fixed command whose code changed (`command-changed`), and whether it was approved since, read back.
- `test/run-database.test.ts` — reading an open agent's own SQLite database (`readRunDatabase()`, in a worker thread): the tables that are there and the queries that need them, read-only, a database it cannot read, a view or a generated column in a table it reads, a read that does not end in time given up while the server goes on, no second read of a database while one has not answered, a link or a named pipe as the database or a journal beside it never opened (not on Windows), and a process with a read that never ends still ending, on its way out and on an uncaught exception (`exitAfterRunDatabaseReads()`, `exitOnUncaughtException()`, with `process.kill` and `process.exit` spied on); and the scrub's opening (`openRunDatabase()`) refusing a rollback journal left over, a generated column, an index on an expression or with a condition and a write-ahead log over 1 GiB, running no checks, and dropping planted triggers and views.
- `test/workspace.test.ts` — git plumbing against real temporary repositories (auto init, snapshots that leave the repo untouched and leave out the CLIs' own homes inside it, also named through a link, HEAD as a snapshot's parent and none without a commit, each file's status and line counts from one diff (names starting with a colon, binary files), worktrees, apply, conflicts, CRLF / non-UTF-8 content applied byte for byte (also when the user's git converts line endings, as Git for Windows' `core.autocrlf` does), long and unusual file lists, kept snapshot refs, repositories that are too broad). Also a repository's own config (its fsmonitor, filters, textconv, one through an include) running nothing in Harness Relay's git commands, nor the drivers of a repository at a submodule (a real submodule still taken), no missing object of a partial clone fetched, and a `core.worktree` naming another folder refused, while the user's own drivers (git-lfs's) are left alone. Checking a patch without changing anything, then applying it, and a diff's paths named after a folder.
- `test/process.test.ts` — process helpers: byte-exact output, stopping subprocesses that ignore SIGTERM (also after the CLI exited, and on shutdown), a missing working directory, relative executable paths, an npm shim's Node script started on Windows with its arguments as they are, and on macOS and Linux npm's link to a Node script started with the node beside it when the PATH has none; a fixed shell script (`runShellScript`) with its flags and the values it reads from its environment, one that runs too long, and a missing shell; a detached program (`spawnDetached`) that outlives the call and is left out of `killAll`, and one that does not exist; children left attached on Windows so a hidden console is inherited (`detachedChild`), in a process group elsewhere; and on Windows only, a real Windows PowerShell script file and command run with their output and exit code. Also `runSync`: a quick probe run directly with its input, killed after its timeout even when it ignores SIGTERM, and `sleepSync`. Two pipes on fds 3 and 4, as a browser's debugging pipe needs them. And `recordSpawns` (for `scripts/cli-check.ts`): a recorder told what a process was started with, what went through its stdin, stdout and stderr and how it ended, nothing once it is unset, and one that throws, as a process starts, as it writes or as it ends, never stopping the process nor keeping its run from ending.
- `test/process-console.test.ts` — the flags a child is actually started with, `spawn` stood in for and `process.platform` flipped: on Windows, `runProcess`, `runShellScript` and `spawnInteractive` are attached (`detached: false`) and hidden (`windowsHide: true`), with piped or ignored stdio so the hidden console is not dropped, and `spawnDetached` stays detached so the desktop updater outlives the server; on macOS and Linux the same calls stay in their own process group.
- `test/winLaunch.test.ts` — what is started on Windows for a CLI: found as a shell finds it (the PATH in PATHEXT's order, whatever the letter case of `Path`; of `Path` and `PATH`, the one Node passes on; a `.JS` PATHEXT names passed over); a program as it is; one not found said so, not started bare; an npm shim's program, or its Node script with the Node beside it, the PATH's or Harness Relay's (npm's current and older shims), none in the desktop app; a shim whose script or program is not there, said so; a script of the user's own with a shim-like line, a shim for another interpreter and one that runs two things, not taken for shims; Cursor's newest whole version folder by date and build time, with `CURSOR_INVOKED_AS` and the compile cache, and another script beside such folders not taken for its launcher; Muse Code's launcher (`muse.cmd`, as its installer writes it) started as it starts Muse, the `muse-bin-<version>.exe` its `.muse-version` names, and refused when that names no binary there or is not a version; a script whose program cannot be told, refused with the reason in both languages; nothing of this off Windows. Also the codespaces count (`POST /codespaces`), on SQLite as D1 is: one more for the day in Taiwan (`dayOf()`, from 16:00 UTC the next), in a table the first count makes (day and count only), with neither the bucket nor GitHub asked and the request's body, user agent and address not kept; a request with an `Origin` header (a browser's, from a web page) 403, uncounted; other methods 405 (`Allow: POST`), other paths the installers' (a query string is the same path); a failing database 503. Also every export of the module a function, but its handler (the Workers runtime takes each for an entry point, and refuses to start on any other), and `wrangler.toml`'s entry, bucket and count database (by its name, no `database_id`: `wrangler deploy` makes it), the bucket the Release workflow uploads to, and `MIRROR` none or an https origin.

Also the two lists of dark colours in `site.css` the same (the system's dark mode and dark chosen), nothing else following `prefers-color-scheme`, the button's icon by theme, each agent's pill text (the `-ink` its helper class gives) 4.5:1 or better on its tint in the light theme and the dark one, with every pill on the pages in an agent's message card or a use case's list of what ran, over the page's background, the bar colours equal to the page's backgrounds on both pages, each page's `<head>` (the landing and use-cases pages) setting the theme from the same key and only to light or dark (in a `try` of its own) and loading the module, and the button the last thing in the header, after GitHub, in both languages and nowhere else.
