The website's use cases ([`site/use-cases/`](../site/use-cases/) and its Chinese page; the home pages list them, and so do the READMEs) are twelve real runs, one for each kind of work, each recorded by `npm run use-cases` ([`scripts/demo/use-cases/`](../scripts/demo/use-cases/)) with the recorder the demo uses (`scripts/demo/recorder.ts`): fixing a bug three ways in parallel worktrees (software engineers), a PRD from attached interviews, with a question the agents ask and the recorder answers (product managers), launch copy from competitors' pages read in Harness Relay's browser, then a branch (marketers), a bank reconciliation done three times over with lead review (accountants), overdue invoices chased by an automation with a goal over two runs (business owners), a brief before a customer call from an automation that waits for the calendar and opens a new chat for it (account managers), a literature review with sources (researchers), a quiz tried on a student (teachers), a contract redlined on your own model server (lawyers), an alert that starts a new chat through a webhook (on-call engineers), a review of attached screenshots (designers) and an app's strings translated, translated back and checked by Antigravity, Copilot and Claude Code when the English file changes, with a note sent mid-reply (localization teams). Record one again when the UI it passes through changes. It needs what the demo needs; the launch copy and the invoices need Claude Code and Codex only, the translation needs Antigravity and Copilot besides Claude Code (installed and signed in, as `agy` and `copilot` on the `PATH`, or in the homes `COPILOT_HOME` names; Antigravity with `GEMINI_API_KEY` set, its only sign-in in Harness Relay), and the contract review needs a model server of your own (below).

```bash
npm run use-cases                                    # record all twelve in both languages, share, cut and publish them
npm run use-cases -- record --dry                    # each start page and setup, no agents (a minute; in folders apart)
npm run use-cases -- share --case write-a-quiz       # share its last recording again (its last share's gists deleted)
npm run use-cases -- record --case write-a-quiz      # one step, one use case (repeatable), or one language (--lang)
npm run use-cases -- record --parallel 2             # two use cases at once (both languages of each always record at once)
npm run use-cases -- cut publish --case write-a-quiz # cut and publish the last recording again
USE_CASES_ENDPOINT_URL=http://homelab:8000/v1 USE_CASES_ENDPOINT_KEY=… \
  npm run use-cases -- record --case contract-review # the contract review, on your own server
```

1. **record** builds the UI and serves its servers a copy of it (in `data/use-cases/`, so that an `npm run build` meanwhile never reloads a page it records), then for each use case and language makes its working directory anew under `/tmp/hr-demo/<lang>/` (`--root`: short, outside this repository, the same every time): a project whose tests fail (`bill-split`), a vendor's draft contract and a playbook in git, a service's logs, deploys and runbook, the briefs and notes the questions point at, and the files to attach beside it (`<folder>-uploads`: interviews, a bank statement and ledger, a chapter, screenshots a headless browser takes of three made-up screens), all made up and the same every time ([`workspaces.ts`](../scripts/demo/use-cases/workspaces.ts)). It starts Harness Relay on a port of its own (8800 and up) with an empty data folder there, without the OS credential store (`RELAY_KEYRING=off`: what a recording saves stays in its database), and sets up what the recording does not show: for the invoices, stand-in IMAP and SMTP servers on this computer (`test/fake-mail-servers.ts`) holding a made-up mailbox (two more mails come in between the runs), the account, and Harness Relay's mail tools for Claude Code and Codex; for the call brief, the same stand-in mail servers and a stand-in CalDAV calendar beside them (the call is added to it after the first check, less than half an hour away), the mail and calendar accounts, and Harness Relay's mail and calendar tools for Claude Code and Codex; for the launch copy, a stand-in web server on this computer serving three made-up competitors' pricing pages, each under a name of its own (`wavecut.localhost`), and Harness Relay's browser for Claude Code and Codex; for the contract review, the model server as an endpoint (`homelab`), its model as Claude Code's, Codex's and Grok's default and as the lead agent's, so that nothing goes to their own services. That server is yours: `USE_CASES_ENDPOINT_URL` (an OpenAI-compatible base URL that also serves Anthropic's Messages API, as vLLM does), `USE_CASES_ENDPOINT_MODEL` (default `qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-unsloth`, the page says so) and `USE_CASES_ENDPOINT_KEY`; without the URL it refuses to record. It records on its own, after the others and one language at a time (`alone` in `cases.ts`): Grok on an endpoint has the endpoint's model in its own `config.toml`, in your Grok home, which every other Grok run writes too. A headless browser then does what a person would ([`cases.ts`](../scripts/demo/use-cases/cases.ts)): the permission, the question (with a file attached, or an automation's message and times), the lead agent's team (confirmed), the answers (a request beyond the permission shown at normal speed and allowed, those after it allowed without slowing down; a question the agents ask answered in the message box when the use case has an answer, else skipped), then what the use case shows: a diff and Apply, a branch, the lead agent's review and a jump to the words it quotes, a file in Files and its folder's ZIP, the outbox's items sent, a second run and the automation's progress, a webhook called with an alert and the bell's notification, an automation that waits for something checked with Check now (the first check only notes what is there; then the call comes near, or the recording changes `locales/en.json` and waits the 30 seconds a file must be still), the new chat it opens and the event it sends, and a note sent while the team replies. Each step leaves a mark in `marks.json`, and `conversation.json` keeps the whole chat.
2. **share** shares each recording's conversation as a person would with Harness Relay's Share ([`share.ts`](../scripts/demo/use-cases/share.ts)): the recording's server is started again on its data folder (so a dry run records in folders of its own, `data/use-cases/.dry/` and `<root>/dry/`, and never removes them), its conversation opened, Share → Create link pressed with the files its replies link to going along (the dialog's default; a reply still running when the recording ended is left out by sharing up to the last finished one). The page goes up as a secret gist with your own `gh` (signed in with the `gist` scope), read on Harness Relay's viewer; `share.json` keeps the link beside the recording, the files attached to its question are copied next to it (`attachments/`), and the gists of the use case's last share are deleted. The shared page holds the replies only, never the tool calls, thinking or permission requests, where a recording's paths and account names show.
3. **cut** speeds the agents' work up so the video lasts about as long as the use case says (75 to 90 seconds) and writes `video.mp4` (1280×800, 24 fps) and `timeline.json`, into `data/use-cases/<case>/<lang>/`.
4. **publish** copies the video to `site/assets/media/use-cases/<case>-<lang>.mp4`, cuts its poster (`.webp`, with its origin in a `.webp.json` sidecar; the same image again keeps both as they were) from the frame the use case names, re-times the use case's chapter rail in its section of the use-cases page, writes when the video was published (kept when it is the same video again) and how long it is into that video's structured data, links the section to its share and to the files attached to its question (`.usecase-links`, under what was asked; the files copied to `site/assets/media/use-cases/files/<case>/<lang>/` and linked on GitHub, which shows them, never as a download), and writes `review.md` next to the recording: the chapters as written, the team, every mark and the replies.

Then read each `review.md` and watch the videos. The chapter sentences are in `cases.ts`; the team, its lead agent, the agent that asked for an approval and the one whose diff was opened are filled in from the recording, but when the agents did something the sentences don't describe, change them there and run `npm run use-cases -- publish`. The pages' other words (whose work it is, what it is, the agents and mode it ran with, what it shows), the home pages' cards and the READMEs' list are written by hand: check them against the recording too, then commit the media, `site/` and the scripts together.

## Releasing

A release is the desktop app's installers for macOS (a `.dmg` for Apple Silicon), Windows (x64 and Arm64 `.exe`) and Linux (x64 and Arm64 `.AppImage`), published together as one GitHub release by the Release workflow ([`.github/workflows/release.yml`](../.github/workflows/release.yml)). It is run by hand; nothing else makes releases or tags.

**Versions** are the day of the release in Taiwan (`RELEASE_TIME_ZONE`, Asia/Taipei), and the git tag is the version itself:

| Version | Is |
| --- | --- |
| `YYYY-MM-DD-rc` | a release candidate of the day's release, published as a pre-release |
| `YYYY-MM-DD-rc.1`, `.2`, … | that day's patches of it |
| `YYYY-MM-DD` | a release |
| `YYYY-MM-DD.1`, `.2`, … | that day's patches of it |
| `YYYY-MM-DD.1-rc`, `YYYY-MM-DD.2-rc`, … | a release candidate of that patch, published as a pre-release |
| `YYYY-MM-DD.1-rc.1`, `.2`, … | that day's patches of it |

A fix on a later day is that day's release, not a patch. A release candidate is always one of the day's next release: the release itself until it is out, then its next patch (`npm run release:next` prints both). Every release needs one: a release is made only once its newest release candidate is out (published by the workflow, not only tagged; an older one does not stand in for a newer one whose run failed), since the [end-to-end tests](#continuous-integration) run for a release candidate only, before it is published. So a release is made on the day of its release candidate: a release candidate of `2026-11-10` made that day does not count for `2026-11-11`, which needs `2026-11-11-rc`. One of a release that is out is refused: it would sort before that release (`2026.1110.0-rc.2` < `2026.1110.0`), and the download page, which offers the newest release by publish time, would show it instead (the app, which goes by version, would never offer it). A release candidate of a patch sorts between the release before it and the patch (`2026.1110.0` < `2026.1110.1-rc` < `2026.1110.1-rc.1` < `2026.1110.1`), so both find the same newest one.

**To release:**

1. On `main`, see today's next versions: `npm run release:next` (it reads the tags on GitHub, and says which release candidate the next release needs out first).
2. In GitHub, open **Actions → Release → Run workflow**, keep **Use workflow from: main**, enter the next release candidate's version, and run it. It runs the end-to-end tests beside the builds, and publishes the release candidate only once they pass.
3. Once it is out (and tried), run the workflow again with the release's version. Made from `main` as it is then: when `main` moved since the release candidate, the version's check warns that the end-to-end tests ran on the candidate's commit, not on what came in since; to have them run on it, release another release candidate first.

`npm run release:check -- <version>` checks a version locally the same way (against your local tags, a release candidate's tag counting as out; `npm run release:check -- <version> --remote` for GitHub's, which asks GitHub through your `gh` whether a release's candidates are out: without the `--`, npm keeps `--remote` for itself), and says which version its notes would start from and which release candidate a release comes after. `npm run release:notes -- <version> --remote` prints the list of changes its notes have by these rules (`--target <commit>` for a release not made yet: a SHA, a tag or a branch on GitHub, `main` rather than `origin/main`), read from GitHub through your `gh`.

**What the notes list.** Every commit that came into `main` since an earlier version, up to the commit the release is made on, whether it was pushed to `main` or merged from a pull request (`scripts/release.ts notes`, `releaseChanges()`): the commits GitHub's comparison of the two lists (`git log <earlier>..<commit>`, the commits a merge brought in included), oldest first, each by its subject. A commit that merged a pull request (squashed, rebased or fast-forwarded, as GitHub records the pull request's merge commit) links to the pull request, without the ` (#N)` a squash ends its subject with, and is by whoever opened it; any other commit links to itself and is by its author. A merge commit in git's words ("Merge pull request …", "Merge branch …", "Merge main into …") says nothing of its own: one that merged a pull request is listed by the pull request's title, any other is left out, since the commits it brought in are listed. A subject stays one line of text: a leading `#`, `>`, `-`, `+`, `*` or `1.` is escaped, and an `@name` in it (outside code and links, with what is attached to it, or in the git name of an author without an account) is put in code, so that the notes notify nobody it merely names. Then the authors with no commit before the version the notes start from, by their account or by an address of theirs in the list (GitHub finds a bot's commits by its address only), as New Contributors, each with their first pull request in the list (else their first change), and the link to the comparison (Full Changelog), as GitHub's own notes have them (a bot as `@name`, without `[bot]`). GitHub asks are made eight at a time, one for each commit's pull request. A list longer than 100,000 characters (GitHub takes 125,000 for a release's notes, the install notes included) ends with how many more changes there are, linked to the history up to the tag (GitHub's comparison page shows 250 commits at most). GitHub's own notes (`--generate-notes`) list pull requests only, and left out what was pushed to `main` straight.

**Where the notes start** (`previousVersion()` in `scripts/release.ts`):

- a release starts at the release before it, so that its notes hold every change since then, its release candidates' included (`2026-11-12` from `2026-11-11.1`, not from `2026-11-12-rc.1`);
- a release candidate starts at the version before it, release or release candidate (`2026-11-12-rc` from `2026-11-11.1`, `2026-11-12-rc.1` from `2026-11-12-rc`).

"Before" goes by version (semver), not by when the tags were made. When there is no such version, the notes list every commit up to the release, from the repository's first.

**What the workflow checks** (`scripts/release.ts`, tested in `test/release.test.ts`), before it builds anything:

- the version follows the rules, with a precise reason when it does not: a `v` in front, `-RC` in capitals, `-rc1` without the dot, `.0`, `.01`, one-digit months or days, a day that is not in the calendar (`2026-02-30`), spaces, or a character that only looks like one (a full-width digit, an en dash, a non-breaking hyphen);
- its date is today in Taiwan, not yesterday or tomorrow (also around midnight UTC, when the two days differ);
- it is the next one: not released already, no patch before the release it patches, no skipped number, a release candidate only of the day's next release (none of a release that is out or of a later patch than the next). The error says which versions today takes;
- a release comes after its newest release candidate (`candidatesOf()`), once that is out: the newest of its day and patch (`2026-11-10.1` needs a `2026-11-10.1-rc…`, the day's release's candidates are not its), which GitHub must have a release of (`hasRelease()`, as for `--own-tag`; any other answer than GitHub's Not Found fails the check). When the newest one's run failed or was stopped (its end-to-end tests, say), it left a tag without a release: the release is refused, an older candidate that is out notwithstanding (main is the newest one's commit, or later), and the error says to run it again or the next one. In the workflow, a release made on another commit than that candidate's tag is warned about (`candidateCommitWarning()`), not refused;
- the run is on `main`;
- the app's update source (the `tokens` job and `desktop/src/updateSource.ts`, tested in `test/desktop-update.test.ts`): first of all, in a job of its own (`tokens`), that the write token is set, and that the read token is not the write token (trimmed, as the build reads them), with a warning when GitHub says both belong to one account, or does not say whose they are (a token expired or revoked, say); then, before anything is installed, that the repository is `owner/name` and not this one, that the read token is a fine-grained one (`updateSourceFrom()`), and that the repository's releases can be read as an app built with them would read them (`repositoryReadable()`: `releases?per_page=1`, with the read token, else with the job's own token in its place; the repository itself answers a token without Contents: Read too). A read token without access to it or to its contents, expired, revoked or waiting for an organization's approval, a private repository without one, or a repository moved (GitHub's 301) stops the run before anything is built; GitHub failing, its limit on requests or no answer only warns, as the copies' check asks again after the builds.

**The tag.** As the run starts, beside the `tokens` job, the `version` job checks the version and tags the commit the run is on with it (its step **Tag the commit**), and at the end the release is published on that tag (`gh release create --verify-tag`), never on one made then. GitHub lets the workflow's token make a ref only on a commit whose `.github/workflows` files are `main`'s as they are (see GitHub Codespaces, below): a tag made once the installers were built was refused (403, "Resource not accessible by integration", with the copies already published) whenever `main`'s workflow files changed while they were built, as they did for 2026-11-17-rc, pushed to `main` 13 seconds after the run started. Made at the start, it is refused only when they changed in the seconds before; the error then names the workflow files that changed, and nothing has been built or published: run the workflow again from `main`. A run that fails after that leaves the tag without a release. The version's checks take such a tag as the workflow's own (`check --own-tag`: GitHub has no release of it, `hasRelease()`; any other answer than GitHub's Not Found fails the check rather than take a release that is out for one), so **Re-run failed jobs** goes on from it, a new run on the same commit leaves it where it is, and a new run of the version on a later commit moves it there (forced). Before publishing, the release job checks that the tag is still on the commit it built. `npm run release:next`, which reads the tags alone, names the version after it meanwhile; the version itself can be run again.

Then it runs the typecheck and the tests, and for a release candidate the end-to-end tests beside the builds (the `e2e` job, which calls `e2e.yml` and passes it no secret; the release job runs only once they passed, or for a release, which skips them), builds the five installers in parallel, the Arm64 ones on GitHub's Arm runners (each runs `desktop/scripts/smoke.ts` on its bundled server first, and `desktop/scripts/check-package.ts` on the packaged app after: fuses, resources, no token in the server's files nor as text in the app's, the repository and the read token it was built with, encoded, and the app's own `--smoke-test`, a question to the fake CLIs included, under `xvfb-run` on Linux), checks the version again against the tags as they are now (a run that started before midnight keeps its day), checks that there is exactly one installer for each end of name in `INSTALLERS` (`site/assets/downloads.js`, which the website and the app's updates look for) and nothing else, copies each one for the app's updates (`updateCopyName()`: `…-mac-arm64-update.dmg` and so on, which the app downloads instead, so that the download counts tell updates from new installs; the website never offers them. Apps released before 2026-11-12-rc still download the installers, and 2026-11-12-rc looks for the copies in the release itself, so their updates count as installs until their users have updated once), checks that every installer was built with the repository and read token the run has now (each build's fingerprint, `sameUpdateSource()`: a run of the failed jobs alone builds nothing again), publishes the copies under the same tag in the repository [`Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay-updates`](https://github.com/Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay-updates) (`UPDATES_REPO`, or the one the variable of that name names, below) first, so that no app is offered the release before its copies are there, then checks that each copy is listed whole where the app will ask for it (`copiesReadable()`: asked with the read token, else the job's own, and downloading nothing, so that no download is counted; a private repository without a read token, or a token that cannot read it, stops the run here, before the release is published; a failure of GitHub's is asked again, three times in all, a 404 once more (a release just made may not be found at once), and its limit on requests, its secondary one too, waited out when all the waits together come within five minutes, else left as a warning; a repository renamed or moved (GitHub's 301, not followed) says to set the variable to its new name; a read token that expires within 90 days is warned about), uploads a release's installers to the download mirror when the website has one and checks that the mirror serves each of them whole (below; a release candidate never goes there), and then publishes the release on the tag it made on the commit it built, with the installers named `Harness-Relay-<version>-<os>-<arch>.<ext>`, notes listing every commit into `main` since the version they start from (above; by the tags of that last check, and listed before anything is published, so that a failure there publishes nothing), and the install notes (the systems each installer needs, the first-run warning on each system, `--no-sandbox` on Ubuntu 24.04, who is offered it: an installed Harness Relay, in its menu and its chat, a release candidate only to release candidates and builds of your own). Only one release runs at a time, and only two jobs may write to the repository: the `version` job, which tags the commit (it checks the code out and installs nothing), and the publishing job, which writes to the copies' repository with a token of its own. After a release (never a release candidate), it points GitHub Codespaces at it (the `latest-release` branch, below). The write token (`UPDATES_TOKEN`) goes to four steps only: the three of the first job, `tokens` (that it is set, the comparison with the read token, and the question of whose they are, each token given to `curl` on its standard input, never among its arguments), which checks nothing out and installs nothing, and the step that publishes the copies, in a job that installs no npm package either. A runner keeps the secrets a job names in its memory for the whole job, so no job that installs packages names it. The read token (`UPDATES_READ_TOKEN`), when there is one, goes to the last two of those, the check of the update source, the app's build and the packaged app's checks, and the release job's two update checks. The comparison comes before anything is built and its artifacts uploaded, which anyone who may read this repository may download. The version reaches the scripts through the environment, never in a command line.

**The copies' token.** The workflow's own token may write to this repository only, so the copies go up with the `UPDATES_TOKEN` secret: a fine-grained personal access token whose resource owner is the owner of the copies' repository (Agentic-System-Lab), for that repository only (`harness-relay-updates`, or the one the `UPDATES_REPO` variable names), with **Contents: Read and write**. Set it with `gh secret set UPDATES_TOKEN --repo Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay` (it asks for the value). Without it, the copies' step stops with an error naming the secret; once it expires, `gh` fails there with GitHub's 401. Either way nothing is published: make a new token and set it again.

**Where the copies are.** An app downloads its updates from the repository the copies go to, built into it (`RELAY_UPDATES_REPO`, [above](#the-desktop-app)): `Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay-updates`, or the one the repository variable `UPDATES_REPO` names (`gh variable set UPDATES_REPO --body <owner/name> --repo Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay`; never this repository, which the build refuses); the check of the update source, the build and the packaged app's checks, the fingerprints' check, the copies' step and the check after it all follow it, read the same way (`updateSourceFrom()`). The versions and their checksums always come from this repository's releases, so wherever the copies cannot be had the app downloads the installer itself, and its update only counts as a new install. That is how the apps built before a change update once it is made: those built for the old repository after the variable names another, and those built without a token once the copies' repository goes private.

To move the copies to another repository:

1. Make the repository, with nothing in it but the copies to come.
2. Let `UPDATES_TOKEN` write to it (or set a new one that may).
3. Set the `UPDATES_REPO` variable to it.
4. If it is private, set `UPDATES_READ_TOKEN` to a token for it (below); if it is public, delete that secret (a token left there would still go into every app).
5. Run the workflow for the next release.

For a private repository, set the `UPDATES_READ_TOKEN` secret as well (`gh secret set UPDATES_READ_TOKEN --repo Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay`, which asks for the value): a fine-grained personal access token (the build refuses any other kind) for that repository only, with **Contents: Read-only** and nothing else beside the Metadata: Read-only that GitHub adds to every token. Never the write token: the workflow refuses `UPDATES_TOKEN` there before it builds anything, and with it anyone could replace the copies (an app then still updates, from the installer, after the copy fails its checksum). If one is set there by mistake all the same, revoke it. A different token that may also write is not caught: check its permissions when you make it.

- **Anyone can read it.** It goes into every installer, where anyone can read it, so it must allow nothing else. **Contents: Read-only** shows the whole repository: keep nothing in it but the copies. The build encodes it (`encodeUpdateSource()`), so that it never stands in the app as a token's text: GitHub revokes a token it finds pushed to a public repository or gist, and anyone could push an unpacked app. That keeps scanners off it, not people.
- **Owner and approval.** Its resource owner must be the repository's owner. For an organization's repository, the organization may have to approve it first (until then it reads nothing), and its policy may cap how long a token lives. Make it with an account of its own, not the write token's: the requests below are counted per account, and anyone with the app can use up that account's with it, which would then hold up publishing the copies with the write token too. The workflow warns when GitHub says both belong to one account, and when it does not say whose they are.
- **Requests.** An app sends it only when it updates, never when it checks for one: one question for the release (`releases/tags/<tag>`) and one for the copy's address, and one more each time a signed address expires during the download. An app built with it does so for a public repository too, the copy then also coming through GitHub's API. These come from the 5,000 requests an hour of the token's account, shared by every installed app.
- **When it stops working.** Once it expires or is revoked, the apps built with it count their updates as new installs until they update to a release built with a new one; they still update. Installed apps keep the token they were built with, so to replace it: set the new secret, publish a release with it while the old one still works, and revoke the old one later (the apps not updated by then count their next update as a new install). If it is misused, or turns out to allow more than reading the copies, revoke it at once: apps keep updating, from the installers. Give it no expiry where the organization allows; the copies' check warns when it expires within 90 days (`expiryWarning()`, from the expiry GitHub gives with an answer).
- **The checks.** Before anything is built, the workflow asks GitHub for the repository's releases with the token, and before it publishes the release, it checks that each copy is listed whole where the app will ask for it, with the token again. Without the secret, a private repository stops the run at the first of these. Once you set or change the secret (or the variable), run the whole workflow again (**Re-run all jobs**, or a new run of the same version): a run of the failed jobs alone keeps the installers built before, which the fingerprint check (`sameUpdateSource()`) refuses before the copies are published. When nothing needs changing (GitHub failing, a copy not uploaded whole, an organization's approval given since), **Re-run failed jobs** is enough; each error of the copies' check says which, the token's saying both, since a token refused may only have waited for that approval. When GitHub's limit on requests keeps the copies' check from finishing, the release is published with a warning: the apps then update from the installers until they can have the copies. A run that stops at the copies' check has already published them under its tag: the next run replaces them, and if that version is dropped, delete them from the copies' repository by hand. To check a token yourself, without it in your shell's history: `read -rs GH_TOKEN && export GH_TOKEN && gh api repos/<owner>/<name>/releases/tags/<tag> --jq '.assets[].name'`.
- **Local builds.** Don't keep `RELAY_UPDATES_TOKEN` or `RELAY_UPDATES_REPO` in your shell's profile: every `npm run desktop` and `npm run desktop:dist` would build them in, and the app would download its updates as they say.

**Download counts.** GitHub counts each installer's downloads (not installs, and repeated downloads count again): new installs on this repository's releases (from the website, its download mirror included, and from the releases page), updates of an installed app on the copies' repository (`harness-relay-updates`, or the one `UPDATES_REPO` names; an update downloaded from the mirror too, which the app counts there itself). GitHub counts requests, not finished downloads: a request for the installer's address on github.com counts, a `HEAD` too and one whose redirect is not followed, and requests to GitHub's CDN at the address it redirects to count as well (not each one: three in a row at one address were seen to count once). A browser's download from GitHub makes both, and was seen to count as 2, as does the mirror's request for it (below). An installed app's update counts once: the app asks for its copy through GitHub's API with its read token (the copies' repository is private), and only the API's address counts, not the CDN's request it redirects to (measured on 2026-11-14 on a release candidate's copies: the API's address alone, its redirect not followed, counted 1, and the API's address with the CDN's first byte after it counted 1 too, 19 minutes on). A count shows up a minute or two later for github.com's request, up to a quarter of an hour later for the CDN's:

```bash
gh api repos/Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay/releases --paginate --jq '.[] | .tag_name as $t | .assets[] | "\($t)\t\(.name)\t\(.download_count)"'
gh api repos/<owner>/<name>/releases --paginate --jq '.[] | .tag_name as $t | .assets[] | "\($t)\t\(.name)\t\(.download_count)"'   # the copies' repository
```

**`npm run stats`** ([`scripts/stats.ts`](../scripts/stats.ts)) puts them together, for maintainers: the releases' downloads by system (macOS, Windows x64 and Arm64, Linux x64 and Arm64), each release's installers as new installs and its copies as updates, in all and release by release, newest first, release candidates and drafts left out. The copies' repository is the workflow's `UPDATES_REPO` variable when `gh variable get` can read it, checked as the build checks it (`updateSourceFrom()`), else `UPDATES_REPO` in `site/assets/downloads.js`; every release's copies are read there, so if it ever moves, the earlier releases' copies stay in the one before. An installer under a past name (2026-11-11's `-mac-universal.dmg`, `PAST_INSTALLERS`) counts for its system; any other file of a release is listed apart, so that a new name never drops downloads silently; a system a release had no file for shows `—`. Beside GitHub's counts it gives about how many downloads they are (`estimate()`): the new installs' totals halved and rounded, as a new install's download was seen to be two requests GitHub counts (`REQUESTS_PER_INSTALL`), and the updates' as they are, an update counting once (`REQUESTS_PER_UPDATE`; both above); each total from GitHub's own, all systems' too, not added up from estimates. Still an estimate: a download tried again counts again, the maintainers' own count too, and a lone request (a `HEAD`, a redirect not followed) counts once; an app that cannot have its copy (from before 2026-11-12, its release candidates included, or whose read token stopped working) updates from the installer, counted as a new install. Then the codespaces count (below), in all and on each of the last seven days in Taiwan. It reads through your own `gh` (or `GH_TOKEN`) and `wrangler` (signed in to the Cloudflare account with the mirror: `npx wrangler login`; started with `npm exec`, which runs it as `npx` does) and writes nothing. A part it cannot read is shown with the reason (exit code 1): the releases, the copies alone (the new installs still shown), or the count; the count's table not made yet reads as none counted. `npm run stats -- --json` gives the same as JSON.

**The download mirror.** GitHub's downloads are slow in parts of the world: from Taiwan (a HiNet connection), its CDN sends an installer at some 0.25 MB/s on one connection (8 to 9 minutes for one of 130 MB) and 1.5 MB/s on six, where the mirror gave 7 to 10 MB/s on one and 5 to 10 MB/s on six. So the website's download buttons, and the app's updates, can use a mirror instead: a Cloudflare Worker ([`mirror/worker.ts`](../mirror/worker.ts), `harness-relay-downloads`) in front of an R2 bucket (`harness-relay-mirror`), at `/<tag>/<file>` as on GitHub, whose address is `MIRROR` in `site/assets/downloads.js` (empty: no mirror, the buttons point at GitHub).

- **What it serves.** A release's five installers only (`Harness-Relay-<tag>` and an end of `INSTALLERS`; nothing else in the bucket is served), as files to save, whole or in byte ranges (a resumed download, `If-Range`), with `GET` and `HEAD` only; and each under its copy's name for the app's updates (an end of `UPDATE_INSTALLERS`), the same file in the bucket. An installer the bucket does not have (an older release, removed from it), or one it fails to give (R2 out of order while the Worker runs), is redirected to GitHub.
- **Counted on GitHub.** For each download it serves itself, a `GET` of the whole file or of a part from its first byte (not a resumed or later part, a `HEAD` or a `304`), the Worker asks GitHub for the installer's first byte as a download there asks (`Range: bytes=0-0`, the redirect followed to GitHub's CDN, with the visitor's user agent), after the answer has started (`waitUntil`). So GitHub counts it as it counts a download from GitHub, and the counts above stay whole; GitHub not reached, it goes uncounted, and the visitor still gets the file.
- **For the app's updates.** An app built with `MIRROR` set downloads its update from the mirror first, under the copy's name, and counts it on GitHub itself once it has the whole file, against the copy (the desktop app's **Updates**, [above](#the-desktop-app)); the mirror never counts those names, and answers 404 (not in the bucket) or 503 (the bucket failing) instead of a redirect, since a redirect to the installer would count as a new install: the app then downloads from GitHub as before. Apps built before `MIRROR` was set keep downloading from GitHub. The mirror holds releases only, so a release candidate updates from GitHub.
- **On the website.** The page finds the newest release on GitHub as before, then asks the mirror with a `HEAD` whether it has the visitor's installer (`mirrorHas()`; its redirect to GitHub not followed, since GitHub would count that); only when the mirror answers it has it do the links go to the mirror, else they stay on GitHub. The release kept in `localStorage` keeps GitHub's addresses.
- **The codespaces count.** GitHub does not tell a repository's owner how many codespaces people make of it, so a codespace the README's or the website's links make says it was made, once (`.devcontainer/count.sh`, its `postCreateCommand`, which runs after the codespace has been made for someone and never in a prebuild; only this repository's, by `GITHUB_REPOSITORY`, on `latest-release`, so not a contributor's on `main` or a branch, nor a fork's; not again on a rebuild, by a mark in `data/`; never outside Codespaces, nor with `DO_NOT_TRACK` set; with a short time limit, as the server's first start waits for it): an empty `POST /codespaces` to `MIRROR`. The Worker adds one to that day's row (the day in Taiwan, as releases go) in a D1 database, `harness-relay-counts` (`COUNTS` in `wrangler.toml`), with nothing else; Workers Logs (`[observability]`) keeps those requests a few days as it keeps the downloads (3 on the free plan, 7 on a paid one). What it sends and how to turn it off is in [Try it in GitHub Codespaces](codespaces.md#what-is-counted). A request with an `Origin` header is refused (403): a browser sends one from a web page, so no page can have its visitors count. Anyone else can still send the request, so it is a count of what was reported, not a proof. To read it: `npm run stats` (above: in all, and each of the last seven days), or `npx wrangler d1 execute harness-relay-counts --remote --config mirror/wrangler.toml --command "SELECT day, created FROM codespaces ORDER BY day"` (`SELECT SUM(created) FROM codespaces` for all of them). D1's free plan takes 100,000 writes a day.
- **In the Release workflow.** For a release (never a release candidate, which the website does not offer), when `MIRROR` is set: the installers uploaded to the bucket under the release's tag before the release is published (the runner's AWS CLI, at R2's S3 address), then each one's last byte asked of the mirror (`curl -r -1`, a redirect not followed: neither counts on GitHub), which must come from the bucket, before the release is published; after publishing, all but the newest two releases by version removed from the bucket (the one before may still be offered by a page that kept it, or be downloading), which keeps it within R2's free storage (10 GB; a release's five installers are some 650 MB). The upload and the pruning get the bucket's token; this job installs no npm package.

To set it up, once, with a Cloudflare account (R2 needs a payment method on file, though this stays within its free tier: storage above, no charge for what is downloaded, ten million reads a month):

1. In the Cloudflare dashboard, **R2 → Create bucket**, named `harness-relay-mirror` (`bucket_name` in `mirror/wrangler.toml`, `MIRROR_BUCKET` in the workflow), or `npx wrangler r2 bucket create harness-relay-mirror --location wnam`. Its location: **Western North America**. Cloudflare's free plan served the Worker to a HiNet connection in Taiwan from San Jose (`cf-ray` ending in `SJC`, where its own speed test is served from Taipei), and from there a bucket in Western North America read about as fast on one connection and two to three times as fast on six (the app's updates) as one in Asia-Pacific. A bucket's location is set the first time its name is created: deleting it and creating it again under the same name keeps the old location, so a new location needs a new name.
2. Deploy the Worker: `npx wrangler login`, then `npx wrangler deploy --config mirror/wrangler.toml`. It prints its address, `https://harness-relay-downloads.<subdomain>.workers.dev` (a custom domain can take its place, as a route). The first deploy also makes the codespaces count's D1 database (`database_name` in `wrangler.toml`, which needs no `database_id`), and the Worker makes its table at the first count. Deploy again after a change to `mirror/worker.ts` or to the names in `site/assets/downloads.js` it imports (`INSTALLERS`, `UPDATE_INSTALLERS`).
3. **R2 → Manage API tokens → Create API token**: **Object Read & Write**, for that bucket only. Set its access key ID and secret access key, and the account's S3 address it shows (`https://<account id>.r2.cloudflarestorage.com`): `gh secret set MIRROR_ACCESS_KEY_ID --repo Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay`, `gh secret set MIRROR_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY --repo Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay` (each asks for the value) and `gh variable set MIRROR_ENDPOINT --body https://<account id>.r2.cloudflarestorage.com --repo Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay`.
4. Put the Worker's address in `MIRROR` in `site/assets/downloads.js` and commit it: the Pages workflow publishes the website with it, and the next release's run uploads its installers. The apps built from then on download their updates from the mirror (from the update to the release after theirs). Until a release is on the mirror, the website keeps GitHub's links (the mirror says it does not have them). With `MIRROR` set and the secrets or the variable missing, a release's run stops before it publishes anything.

To stop using it, empty `MIRROR`: the website goes back to GitHub's links, the workflow uploads nothing, and codespaces are no longer counted. Apps built with it still ask the mirror first: keep the Worker running (an empty bucket is enough: it answers 404, and they download from GitHub), or delete it, and they download from GitHub when it cannot be reached. To check it by hand without counting a download: `curl -sI <MIRROR>/<tag>/<file>` (200 from the bucket, 302 to GitHub without it).

**GitHub Codespaces.** The README's and the website's Codespaces links open the `latest-release` branch (`codespaces.new/Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay/tree/latest-release`, `CODESPACES_BRANCH` in the workflow; tested in `test/codespaces-release.test.ts`), so that a codespace runs the newest release, not `main` as it is. After a release is published (never a release candidate), the workflow's step **Point GitHub Codespaces at the release** moves the branch to the commit the release was made on (forced), or makes it there when GitHub says there is none (404), with the job's own token. Anything else on that branch is lost at the next release: nobody commits to it. Keep it as long as the links open it: without it they fail until the next release makes it again. The ruleset `latest-release: no deletion` keeps anyone from deleting it (with no one let past it: it takes nothing from the workflow, which only moves the branch), and nothing else: anyone with write access can still push to it. GitHub lets the workflow's token point a branch only at a commit whose `.github/workflows` files are `main`'s as they are now: tried on this repository on 2026-11-14, the token moved a branch from release 2026-11-13 to `main`'s tip across the workflow changes between them, and made one there, but was refused (403, "Resource not accessible by integration") a branch at a commit whose workflows differ from `main`'s, made or moved. So the step fails when `main`'s workflow files changed while the run built. Then, as on any other failure, the release stays out, codespaces keep opening the release before it, and the step says so as an error (in the run's summary too) without failing the run, with the command to move the branch by hand (`gh api -X PATCH repos/Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay/git/refs/heads/latest-release -f sha=<the release's commit> -F force=true`, or `-X POST …/git/refs -f ref=refs/heads/latest-release -f sha=…` when there is none yet), run with a token that may change workflows (your own `gh`'s). A codespace made before keeps its release; [Try it in GitHub Codespaces](codespaces.md) says how to get a newer one.

**Codespaces prebuilds.** A codespace made from the links starts from a prebuild of `latest-release` when GitHub has one for its region: GitHub runs the dev container ahead of time, up to and including `updateContentCommand` (the base image, the features, `setup.sh`, `npm ci` and the build), and keeps the result; a codespace made from it runs only `postCreateCommand` (`count.sh`, never in a prebuild) and `postAttachCommand`. GitHub runs `onCreateCommand` only when a prebuild is first made, and `updateContentCommand` again at each update of it, so `setup.sh` runs in `updateContentCommand`, before `npm ci`: each update gets the CLIs' newest versions and the script as it is (it can run over an earlier run of itself). By default, while an update runs or after one failed, codespaces are made from the prebuild before it. Without a prebuild (in a region it is not made in, on `main`, another branch or a fork, in a dev container on your own computer) the codespace is made from the start: about four minutes on 2026-11-14, of which 69 s pulled the base image (most of it unpacking), 18 s installed the features, 86 s ran `setup.sh` and 26 s `npm ci` and the build. Run again in one codespace, `setup.sh` took 44–46 s as it was then, and 29–33 s with npm's install beside the makers' installers. A repository admin sets the prebuild up in **Settings → Codespaces → Set up prebuild** (GitHub documents no API for it): branch `latest-release`, configuration `.devcontainer/devcontainer.json`, trigger **Every push** (the branch moves only at a release), all regions, the 2 versions GitHub keeps by default, and the maintainers named for its failure notifications. Its runs are in **Actions**, as **Codespaces Prebuilds**. It is billed to the organization (or its enterprise), which needs a payment method and a spending limit for Codespaces: each version kept, in each region, as codespace storage, and each run's Actions minutes. A prebuild gets the repository's and the organization's Codespaces secrets (not a user's), and what `setup.sh` writes into the home folder is in every codespace made from it: give this repository no Codespaces secrets of either kind. The workflow moves the branch with its own token, and what a workflow's `GITHUB_TOKEN` pushes starts no workflow run; GitHub's documentation does not say whether that holds for a prebuild. So after a release, check that a **Codespaces Prebuilds** run started for it and that its log shows `setup.sh`'s output; if none started, set the trigger to **Automation** (daily) instead. After the first prebuild, check in a codespace made from it that `git symbolic-ref --short HEAD` prints `latest-release`, which `count.sh` needs to count it.

## Debugging tips

- **Server logs** — the `[server]` lines in the `npm run dev` output; adapter failures include the CLI's stderr tail in the message's error.
- **SSE** — `curl -N -b 'relay_session=<cookie>' 'http://127.0.0.1:8789/api/conversations/<id>/events?key=<key>'` shows the raw event stream under `npm run dev` (8787 under `npm start`; the port its `[dev]` line names when 8789 was in use, and the cookie `relay_session_<port>` when Vite took another port than 5173). Take the `relay_session` cookie and the key (`relay:key` in localStorage) from a signed-in browser's developer tools.
- **Language** — add `-H 'X-Relay-Lang: en'` to a `curl` call to see the English errors and templates.
- **Auto team** — the lead agent's run is a normal CLI session in the conversation's working directory; its reply and its cost are on the plan message (`messages.plan`, `cost_usd`).
- **Reproduce a CLI call** — the adapters' argument lists in `src/server/agents/*.ts` can be run by hand; add `--debug` (Claude, Grok) to see more.
- **Worktrees** — `git -C <working dir> worktree list` and `git branch --list 'relay/*'` show what parallel conversations created; `data/worktrees/<conversation>/` holds the checkouts.
- **Voice input** — `GET /api/voice` shows what is installed and the last install's log; the engine runs as `whisper-server` (see `ps`) and stops after 10 idle minutes. Downloads go to `data/voice/` (`models/`, and `engine/` on Linux and Windows), or to the folder `RELAY_VOICE_DIR` names; the log shows the address of each download.
- **Stale session** — with the server stopped, delete the agent's row in `agent_sessions` to force a fresh session with full history (open conversations are cached in memory, so edit the database only while the server is down).

## Conventions

- Strict TypeScript; shared types only in `src/shared/types.ts`.
- Never send secrets to the client (`SettingsView` returns masked keys only).
- New write endpoints automatically get the JSON content-type and Host/Origin checks from the `/api/*` middleware — keep it that way.
- Spawned processes go through `process.ts` so they are tracked and killed on shutdown.
